Fabregas: "Siamo nella storia, ma pensiamo solo all'Inter. A San Siro vogliamo..."

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fabregas: "Siamo nella storia, ma pensiamo solo all'Inter. A San Siro vogliamo..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

L'euforia in casa Como è ovviamente alle stelle dopo il 9-1 rifilato al Lens. Cesc Fabregas però, nell'intervista concessa alla Gazzetta dello Sport di oggi, si è già proiettato verso il prossimo impegno di campionato, contro l'Inter a San Siro. Qui uno stralcio delle sue dichiarazioni:

Fabregas, come ci si sente dopo una notte storica?

�È vero, siamo nella storia. E’ stata una serata indimenticabile per i nostri tifosi e per la società. È girato tutto dalla nostra parte, è andato tutto bene, ora però dobbiamo continuare così perché domenica abbiamo un’altra partita importante».

Tra due giorni a San Siro affronterete l’Inter, squadra che il suo Como non ha mai battuto. Che cosa chiederà ai ragazzi?

�È giusto goderci questo momento perché il gruppo se lo merita, ma il calcio viaggia a velocità altissima. Ai ragazzi ho già detto che dobbiamo archiviare Lens e pensare solo all’Inter. Ci aspetta una partita durissima in casa dei campioni d’Italia in carica, sarà una sfida molto dispendiosa dal punto di vista fisico e mentale, abbiamo poco tempo ma dobbiamo prepararla al meglio. L’obiettivo è andare a Milano per mostrare la nostra vera identità. Sarà la nona partita di questo percorso tra una sosta e l’altra per le nazionali. Dobbiamo cercare di finire bene, imparando a voltare pagina».

Quattro gol in casa nel debutto in Champions con il Lipsia e altri quattro al Manchester United, ora nove nel primo successo europeo in trasferta: innegabile che i numeri dell’attacco in Europa facciano paura. Come se li spiega?

�Nessuno poteva aspettarsi un 9-1, un risultato che non esiste nel calcio, una cosa così al massimo può capitarti una volta nella vita. Sono orgoglioso dei miei ragazzi, i numeri sono ottimi ma siamo solo all’inizio del nostro percorso in Champions League: abbiamo giocato quattro partite, con il Lens abbiamo disputato una buona gara anche se nei primi 5 minuti del secondo tempo siamo stati un po’ superficiali. Però abbiamo interpretato bene la fase difensiva oltre a quella offensiva, anche se la rete subita mi ha fatto arrabbiare: con i tre cambi avevamo modificato qualcosa dietro ma il mio ex compagno Aguilar ha fatto un gran gol».

Qual è la cosa che le ha dato più soddisfazione?

�Un risultato così ruba l’occhio, però a me piace vedere la squadra che prova sempre a dare tutto. Abbiamo rispettato il piano gara andando costantemente in avanti e sempre con la nostra idea di gioco. È questo che mi rende felice, perché abbiamo giocato rispettando l’avversario fino all’ultimo minuto ma pressando e cercando di fare il nostro calcio. I ragazzi mi hanno fatto impazzire».

What I learned building a Mac Dock that switches with my work

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I built HeyDock to give different kinds of work their own Mac Dock. My Writing mode and Building mode can have different apps, sites, and widgets. Here are two implementation details that mattered once I started using it as a daily Dock.

A browser can be where you write, debug, or get distracted. The app name alone is a poor signal. HeyDock checks the rules the user set first, then its local cache. Only after those checks does it ask Apple’s on-device model, if the model is available. If Apple Intelligence is off, the rules and cache still work. Users can also correct a verdict directly.

The model is asynchronous, so a result can arrive after the user has switched windows. HeyDock checks that the subject is still in front before updating the Dock’s indicator. Without that check, the UI can describe the window you just left.

HeyDock can sit next to the system Dock. Full replacement is an option. In that mode, the app saves the system Dock’s existing auto-hide preferences before hiding it and restores those settings when HeyDock quits. The app also checks for preferences left behind by an interrupted session so there’s a recovery path.

HeyDock is available as a one-time purchase for Macs with Apple silicon running macOS 26 or later. The product site has the demo and details.

This write-up was drafted with AI assistance and checked against the implementation.

AWX OAuth Scopes and RBAC Both Decide What Automation Can Do

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AWX centralizes inventory, credentials, projects, and job execution. Its API can launch automation and change the objects that store credentials. OAuth tokens carry read and write scopes, while AWX role-based access control limits the resources those scopes can reach. Reviewing only the token scope, or only the role, leaves half of the authorization decision unexamined.

AWX's token documentation describes OAuth 2 access tokens and explains that read and write scopes provide a mask over the RBAC permission system. RBAC documentation describes delegation across organizations, inventories, projects, templates, and credentials.

A token with write scope is not automatically allowed to modify every object. A highly privileged role is still constrained by the token scope. The effective permission is the intersection. Test both dimensions with a non-production object before granting a production token.

Create one OAuth application or token per integration and record the owning organization, user, scopes, and last use. A deployment pipeline that launches a known template does not need permission to edit credentials or create new projects.

Review credentials attached to job templates. API access to a template may reveal its configuration or allow execution under a stored credential. The ability to launch automation can be as sensitive as direct access to the target system.

AWX administration documentation describes commands for revoking OAuth2 tokens for a user or clearing revoked tokens. Build revocation into offboarding and incident response. Confirm that revoked tokens fail and that refresh tokens cannot recreate access.

Rotate tokens after role changes. A user or service account that loses an organization role should not retain an unrelated long-lived token that can still reach another resource.

The count-only queries used sub_type=all, page 1, and page size 1. app="AWX" returned 9, title="AWX" returned 9,788, and http.body="AWX" returned 966,935.

The body count is especially broad because AWX is a short acronym. The result can include unrelated products, news, and documentation. The application fingerprint is much smaller and more specific, but it does not establish whether a deployment is public, configured, or vulnerable.

Treat all three values as inventory leads, not an exposed-control-plane census. Compare them with owned domains, certificates, and AWX deployment records.

Map each token to the jobs it can launch, the inventories it can target, and the credentials those jobs can use. Review webhook and notification integrations that can trigger jobs without a human. Confirm that project updates cannot introduce an unreviewed playbook into a privileged template.

AWX is designed to concentrate automation authority. The token review should identify not only who can call the API, but which systems can be changed when that call succeeds.

T3 Code: Control All Your AI Coding Agents From Your Phone

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at devpik.com

T3 Code lets you control the AI coding agents on your machine from your phone. It works with Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, OpenCode and more, uses the subscriptions you already pay for, and it had 45,475 stars when we checked.

T3 Code calls itself an "agent harness control surface", which is a grand way of saying one thing: it is the remote control for the AI coding agents running on your machine. You open an app on your iPhone, your Android phone, the web or a desktop app, and you drive the agents that are already set up on your computer, from wherever you are.

It works with the subscriptions you already have. Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Grok Build, OpenCode, Google Antigravity and fx are all supported, and if a provider is installed and authenticated on your machine, T3 Code can control it. There is no new subscription to buy and no new model to pay for. The README asks the question itself, "Wait, what are you selling me?", and answers it in one word: "Nothing."

The project is written in TypeScript, licensed under MIT, and it had 45,475 stars when we checked on November 4, 2026, after gaining roughly 12,500 stars in the past week. The team says they built it because they wanted the best possible development experience with agents, inspired by the Codex desktop app, Conductor, Claude Desktop and Cursor Glass, but none of those met their bar for something performant, remote-ready and truly open. If the project ever goes the wrong direction, they want you to have everything you need to fork it and build the editor you want.

The practical appeal is consolidation. If you use Claude Code for one project, Codex for another and Cursor somewhere else, each normally has its own interface and its own way of being driven. T3 Code puts them behind one control surface, so you check on runs, steer agents and keep working from your phone instead of being chained to the machine the agents run on.

Setup follows the provider, not T3 Code. Install and authenticate at least one provider before use: Codex with the Codex CLI and codex login, Claude with Claude Code and claude auth login, Cursor with the Cursor CLI and agent login, Grok Build with grok login, OpenCode with opencode auth login, and fx with fx login on the machine running the T3 Code server. Antigravity is the exception: enable it in Settings, then use Install Antigravity and Sign in with Google, with no CLI required.

There is also a docs folder in the repository covering the details that matter once you are running: permission modes, keyboard shortcuts, project settings, appearance preferences, remote access from a phone or another machine, connecting agents over MCP, source control integrations, multiple accounts per provider, and running T3 Code as a background service. There is no docs site yet, so the repository docs are the documentation.

The fastest route is the install script, then the t3 command to start the server and open the local web app:

On Windows, the PowerShell equivalent is irm https://t3.codes/install.ps1 | iex. Once installed, t3 service install keeps the server running in the background, t3 update moves to a newer release, and t3 --help has the full reference. To try it once without installing anything, run npx t3@latest instead.

Prefer a desktop app? The latest release is on GitHub Releases, and the package managers are covered: winget install T3Tools.T3Code on Windows, scoop install extras/t3code, brew install --cask t3-code on macOS, a .deb for Debian and Ubuntu, t3code-bin or t3code-nightly-bin from the AUR on Arch, and a nixpkgs package that includes both the desktop app and the t3 command. The mobile apps are on the App Store and Google Play, and the web app lives at app.t3.codes.

The README is blunt about the project's age, and you should take it seriously: "We are very very early in this project. Expect bugs." A control surface for your coding agents sits in a privileged position, so running an early project here deserves more caution than installing a text tool. Keep it on a machine you can afford to babysit, and read the permission modes doc before you hand an agent a long leash. Supervising from a phone also makes it easy to approve work you have only skimmed, so read the actual changes at a desk before you merge: a text diff shows every line an agent rewrote, and the AI Code Detector flags the placeholder scaffolding and debug leftovers agents tend to leave behind.

Contributions are mostly closed for now. The team says small fixes may be considered, but big features will not be, so if you adopt T3 Code hoping to shape its roadmap, expect to wait. Building from source is also a heavier lift than usual: it uses Vite+ and needs the global vp command-line tool, with full instructions in the repository's contributing guide.

None of that cancels the core idea. If you already pay for two or three agent subscriptions and you want to supervise them away from your desk, a single open source remote control with no subscription of its own is a genuinely useful shape. Just go in knowing it is early, and let the phone app earn your trust before you give it the keys to anything important.

An open source control surface for the AI coding agents on your machine. It lets you drive those agents from an iPhone, Android phone, web app or desktop app, instead of sitting at the machine they run on.

Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Grok Build, OpenCode, Google Antigravity and fx. Each provider must already be installed and authenticated on your machine before T3 Code can control it.

No. T3 Code works with the subscriptions you already have for Claude Code, Codex, Cursor and the rest. The README's own answer to what they are selling is: nothing. It is MIT licensed open source.

Run curl -fsSL https://t3.codes/install.sh | sh, then run t3 to start the server and open the local web app. Windows uses a PowerShell installer, desktop apps are on GitHub Releases and the major package managers, and npx t3@latest tries it once without installing.

Yes, that is the point of the project. The iOS and Android apps, plus the web app at app.t3.codes, connect to the T3 Code server on your machine so you can supervise and steer agents remotely.

Not yet, by the team's own account. The README says the project is very early and to expect bugs, and contributions are mostly not being accepted yet beyond small fixes.

Your Churn Benchmark Is Lying to You in a Specific, Fixable Way

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Somewhere in your last board deck there is a churn benchmark: an industry average, sourced from a respectable report, against which your number is pronounced fine or alarming. This note is not an argument against benchmarks — it is an argument for reading the one you have with the suspicion it deserves, because churn benchmarks fail in a specific and predictable way: they average together businesses whose churn is made of entirely different stuff, and then invite you to compare your stuff to the average.

Churn decomposes into voluntary exits (decisions) and involuntary ones (payment accidents), and the ratio between them varies wildly by model. A B2B tool with annual contracts and corporate cards might lose almost nobody involuntarily; a consumer subscription on monthly billing with debit cards can lose more people to failed payments than to every competitor combined. When a benchmark blends those worlds into "media subscriptions average 6%," it has produced a number that describes no actual business, and comparing yourself to it can mislead in both directions — complacency when your voluntary churn is terrible but your involuntary is low, panic when the reverse holds.

Benchmarks also blend definitions: customer churn against revenue churn, monthly against annualized, trials counted or not. Before any comparison, force the two questions — which churn, computed how — and refuse to proceed until the benchmark answers them or admits it cannot. A surprising share cannot, at which point the number is decoration and should be treated as such in the deck.

Two things. First, existence proofs: a benchmark table broken out by industry and model can reveal a loss category you have not been counting at all — if businesses like yours routinely lose 2% a month to payment failure and your reports show no such line, the gap is in your accounting, not your performance. Second, orders of magnitude: knowing whether a problem is a 0.2% problem or a 3% problem in your model class decides whether it deserves a meeting. A benchmark set organized by industry, with the sources named and the definitions stated is built for exactly those two uses; treat its finer distinctions as hypotheses about your business, never as grades.

Your own history, decomposed. Last year's involuntary share against this year's, voluntary exits by stated reason over time, recovery rates by class — these comparisons survive scrutiny because the definitions are constant and the composition is yours. External benchmarks can start that conversation once a year. The internal series should be running it every month.

The most expensive benchmark error is not analytical at all; it is managerial. A leader reads that top-quartile businesses in the category hold churn under some line, sets that line as the team's target, and thereby commits the roadmap to a number whose composition nobody examined. If the line was achieved in businesses with a fraction of your involuntary share — different cards, different geographies, annual billing — the team will spend the year chasing voluntary-churn programs for what is substantially a payments problem, or vice versa, and will be judged against a target that was never reachable by the route they were funded to take. Targets should be set from your decomposed ledger outward: this much voluntary reduction from these programs, this much involuntary reduction from that recovery work. The benchmark can witness the sum. It should never dictate it.

Compare to learn what exists. Measure yourself to learn what is true. The first is a library; the second is an instrument, and businesses are steered by instruments.

Benchmark literacy and retention data notes, from Swipe Again.

AI agents are your new customers. I built a checker to see which stores they can actually read

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at fahadazhar.site

A shopper asks ChatGPT for "a waterproof hiking jacket under $150 that ships to Dubai". The answer gets built from whatever the agent could read in the few seconds it spent on your store. If it couldn't read you, you aren't in the answer. Nobody tells you that you lost the sale.

I kept getting the same question from store owners: "Are we visible to these things or not?" Guessing wasn't good enough, so I built a checker that fetches a store the way an agent does and scores what it finds.

Once you strip it down, it comes to 8 checks. They don't all count the same:

llms.txt / agents.md get reported but weigh 0. Nobody has shown agents rely on them yet, and I didn't want the score to reward hype.

Plenty of stores added blanket AI blocks in 2024 to stop training scrapers, and they took out the search bots in the same line. Training bots and shopping-answer bots are different user agents:

The checker looks at 3 agents: OAI-SearchBot, PerplexityBot and Googlebot. Training blocks get a note in the report but don't lower the score.

A public "enter any URL" tool turns into an SSRF tool unless you lock it down:

It samples up to 3 product pages, not one. A single product page lies too often: one product has rich data, the next has nothing.

On a first run, a big store scored badly on "product data". The JSON-LD was there. Shopify had answered my second request with a 429 (rate limited), and my code read the empty body as "no structured data".

Now a 429 gets polite retries with backoff. If the page still won't load, the report says "didn't load" as a warning instead of failing the store. A checker that confidently tells you wrong things is worse than no checker at all.

Each of these takes under an hour to fix. The problem is that nobody checks.

The checker is free and needs no signup: paste a store URL and you get a score with the exact fix for each failed check.

If you build stores for clients, run it on one of yours and tell me in the comments what it caught. I'm still tuning the weights.

Zcash’s major network upgrade NU7 goes live on mainnet, cutting block time by threefold

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

NU7 also activates the Network Sustainability Mechanism, which removes 60% of each block's transaction fees from circulation and stores them in a public reserve.

Zcash Zcash (ZEC) announced that its major network upgrade, NU7, activated on the mainnet at block height 3,543,405 on Nov. 5.

The upgrade reduces the target block interval from 75 seconds to 25 seconds. Block subsidies are scaled down by the same factor so that the rate of new ZEC issuance remains essentially unchanged. The number of blocks between halvings is tripled, which preserves the existing halving schedule and the 21 million ZEC supply cap.

NU7 also activates the Network Sustainability Mechanism. The mechanism removes 60% of each block's transaction fees from circulation and stores it in a public reserve, with miners receiving the remaining 40%. The reserved fees are scheduled for gradual reissuance through block rewards beginning at block 8,939,664, expected to start in 2031.

"Like Bitcoin, Zcash faces a long-term challenge: how to sustain network security as block rewards decline. The NSM helps address that while preserving the 21 million coin cap," said Shielded Labs, an independent organization supporting the development of Zcash. The organization said NSM is its first major mainnet deployment on Zcash.

The upgrade also disallows version 4 transactions. As the original Sprout shielded pool relies exclusively on that format, any ZEC remaining in Sprout becomes unspendable. Approximately 12,224 ZEC sat in the pool at the activation height.

Node operators were required to upgrade software before the activation height to remain on the consensus chain. The changes do not require significant updates for most wallets.

ZEC dropped 5.17% in the past 24 hours to trade at $1,254.7 as of 2:05 a.m. on Friday, amid a broader downturn in the crypto market. The GMCI 30 index, measuring the performance of the top 30 cryptocurrencies, is down 6.9%.

“Nemici mai, avversari sì”: Zanetti torna al Penzo, Nicola ritrova l’Udinese

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Da alfieri del passato a rivali nella sfida più importante. Si può riassumere così Venezia-Udinese che aprirà l'undicesima giornata di Serie A, per gli allenatori protagonisti della sfida: Davide Nicola sulla panchina lagunare e Paolo Zanetti su quella friulana.

Un accostamento, quella fra tecnico e squadra d'appartenenza, che stona. Almeno per il momento. Almeno per un altro po' di tempo. Perché entrambi hanno un passato che li lega proprio alla società che questa sera cercheranno di battere. Nicola ha allenato l’Udinese nella stagione 2018/19, mentre Zanetti nel 2021 ha riportato il Venezia in Serie A dopo 19 anni.

I precedenti: Nicola a Udine, Zanetti a Venezia Nicola si sedette sulla panchina dell’Udinese il 13 novembre 2018, al posto di Julio Velazquez, con la squadra terzultima a 9 punti dopo 12 giornate. Il debutto fu vincente, 1-0 alla Roma il 24 novembre, e arrivarono anche due successi consecutivi negli scontri diretti contro Chievo (1-0) e Bologna (2-1). Il bilancio finale fu di 16 punti in 15 gare, con 4 vittorie e 4 pareggi. Il 20 marzo 2019, dopo le sconfitte con Juventus (4-0) e Napoli (4-2), fu esonerato con la squadra a un punto sopra la zona retrocessione e con una gara da recuperare contro la Lazio. Zanetti, invece, ha guidato il Venezia dal 14 agosto 2020 al 27 aprile 2022. Chiuse la stagione di Serie B al quinto posto con 59 punti, poi eliminò Chievo e Lecce ai play-off e il 27 maggio 2021 batté il Cittadella in finale (0-1 all’andata, 1-1 al ritorno), riportando i lagunari in A dopo 19 anni. In Serie A chiuse il girone d’andata in zona salvezza, poi arrivarono otto sconfitte consecutive: fu esonerato con la squadra ultima a 22 punti in 33 partite. Tra queste ci fu anche il 3-0 subito a Udine alla seconda giornata.

Zanetti e il ritorno al Penzo Alla presentazione, Zanetti, in veste di neo tecnico dell’Udinese ha dedicato ampio spazio al suo passato in laguna: "Il legame con quella città ci sarà sempre ed è innegabile, sono già tornato altre volte da avversario, ma nemici mai, avversari sì". Sul Venezia ha spiegato: "Il Venezia è una squadra come noi, ha fatto investimenti importanti, ha raccolto meno di quanto seminato e ha un potenziale inespresso che prima o poi verrà fuori". Conosce bene anche l’ambiente: "Sarà una gara difficile, conosco bene il Penzo, è piccolino ma caldo, c’è grande passione, dobbiamo essere pronti a stare dentro la partita". Zanetti ha poi ridimensionato il peso della sfida: "Una partita importante, ma se venerdì si vince non siamo salvi e se si perde non siamo retrocessi".

Safari's Autofill feature may be distoring time

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Repeated reports about inaccurate form filling have lingered for years

The autofill feature of Apple's Safari browser appears to be suffering from a date handling error that occasionally leads it to supply an incorrect credit card expiration date when filling out forms.

The supposed issue has been a sore spot for Apple customers dating back at least to 2017, when the author of an Apple Community forum post complained about changes to the month field in the Autofill credit card expiration date when moving between time zones.

"I've noticed for the last year or so, whenever I travel from Eastern time zone (my home) to Central time zone or even Pacific time zone that my credit card expiration date month changes in Safari Autofill," the Safari user wrote.

Another post in 2021 raised similar concerns.

"I don't know how to reproduce this," the author wrote. "Every few months Safari on any of my devices changes the expiration month of all of my stored credit cards by decreasing the month by 1, for example instead of 10/2029 it changes it to 09/2029."

Three years later, another person posting to Apple's Community forum reported the same issue.

"For a few years now every time I use Autofill on my computer my credit cards (ALL of them) always have their expiration dates change one month earlier," the 2024 post says. "I thought it was just a glitch on my computer but I bought a new computer and didn't transfer anything from my old one and it is still doing it."

An unrelated Autofill bug report from 2024 suggests that if there is an issue, it resides in Apple-owned Safari code rather than Safari's open source WebKit engine.

Wherever the bug may reside, Safari's unreliable handling of credit card dates has kept a complaint-filled Reddit thread going for four years. And the reported month-shifting has been noted in other threads, too.

The Register was unable to reproduce the described behavior, but a reader insists it's still a problem.

"The Safari AutoFill feature sometimes fills in the credit card expiration date wrong," the reader explained. "My credit card expires in December, but sometimes Apple helpfully enters November."

"To be clear, the card is stored correctly when inspected in Settings," our tipster added. "The off-by-one error only happens when filling out a form in Safari, and not consistently."

One possible way this might happen is if the Safari Autofill code implemented the expiration date using Swift's DateComponent type and stored it as a Calendar object with UTC as the TimeZone identifier. Doing so would anchor the date to a specific time. If the date components were extracted in Pacific Time, the value would be represented as eight hours ahead (PST). So 9/1/2027 at 00:00 would appear as 8/31/2027 at 16:00.

Apple did not immediately respond to a request for comment, but we're told Apple's Help Desk – or "Helpless Desk" as our source put it – advised clearing the browser cache and re-entering details of the affected credit card.

If that works, it suggests Safari's handling of cached data in the context of Autofill dates may need to be revisited.

Apple does not maintain a public bug database and the community-maintained OpenRadar sheds no light on these reports. ®

L'esempio della Juve può essere di aiuto. Dopo il disastro di Marassi il direttore generale Carnevali è sceso pesantemente in campo confrontandosi con Spalletti e i giocatori. Era chiaro a tutti che qualcosa non funzionava dentro lo spogliatoio bianconero. Bastava sfogliare la rassegna stampa e valutare gli sguardi di alcuni calciatori. Carnevali ha scelto di difendere a spada tratta il tecnico di Certaldo. Di non dare alibi al gruppo. E, sarà un caso, ma la Juve è ripartita. E Spalletti non perde occasione per ringraziare il suo Direttore.

La patata bollente ora è passata nelle mani di De Laurentiis. Il Napoli è in picchiata. Il dopo partita di Champions è stato, se possibile, addirittura peggio del comportamento dei partenopei in campo. Polemiche, attacchi incrociati tra gli atleti. Ma il Presidente deve scendere in campo prima confrontandosi con Allegri e con il gruppo. Stabilendo in maniera netta se fare muro intorno al tecnico di Livorno oppure consegnarsi ai suoi giocatori più importanti. Una terza via non esiste.

È evidente che questa è destinata a essere una stagione di passaggio per il Napoli. Allegri non è stata una scelta giusta. De Laurentiis non ha avuto il coraggio di rischiare su un tecnico giovane capace di proporre un calcio fresco, offensivo. Ma anche il gruppo giocatori ormai è logoro. Insomma, a giugno sarà rivoluzione Napoli

‘Wild Horse Nine’ review: John Malkovich and Sam Rockwell shine in Martin McDonagh's dark CIA comedy

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Sign up to get the latest updates on all of your favorite content! From cutting-edge tech news and the hottest streaming buzz to unbeatable deals on the best products and in-depth reviews, we’ve got you covered.

Be AI savvy with your weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editor and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Unlock the vast world of Apple news straight to your inbox. With coverage on everything from exciting product launches to essential software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

Our weekly newsletter is expertly crafted to immerse you in the world of streaming. Stay updated on the latest releases and our top recommendations across your favorite streaming platforms.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

I walked in expecting pretentious Oscar bait — here's what I actually got

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Although his films have been highly acclaimed and nominated for multiple Oscars, I’ve always been slightly skeptical of the work of writer-director Martin McDonagh. He’s undeniably clever, but there’s a show-offy style to his cleverness that bothers me, especially when it’s in service of making self-important social commentary.

So I went into his new film “Wild Horse Nine” with relatively low expectations, just hoping for something less self-serious than “Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri” and less smarmy than “Seven Psychopaths,” his two previous collaborations with star Sam Rockwell.

What I got is probably my favorite McDonagh movie to date, a dark buddy comedy that harkens back to his 2008 debut feature “In Bruges.” It’s still a bit enamored of its own cleverness at times, and never quite as emotionally affecting as it aims to be. But it’s an entertaining showcase for Rockwell and John Malkovich as a pair of dirtbag CIA agents in 1973, gearing up for the forthcoming military coup in Chile.

McDonagh’s social commentary remains glib and not particularly insightful, but the period setting is really just window dressing for a story about a couple of unlikely friends taking stock of their lives.

The movie opens with real-life images of Chileans celebrating the victory of left-wing president Salvador Allende, establishing the historical context before shifting to similarly grainy film footage being shot by CIA agent Lee Chesterfield (Rockwell) on his new camera. He’s playfully interviewing his colleague Chris Carlisle (Malkovich), and McDonagh casually introduces the idea that Chris was involved in the 1963 assassination of U.S. President John F. Kennedy, in an offhand reference that establishes the movie’s cynical sense of humor.

Lee and Chris have spent a year in Chile laying the groundwork for overthrowing Allende’s government, and they’ve been rewarded with a vacation: a week spent on Easter Island, also known as Rapa Nui, the Polynesian island off the coast of Chile famous for its ancient statues of large heads. Lee and Chris bicker and banter on their way to the island, in what at first seems like it’s just a work break.

Chris is remarkably reckless about revealing the truth of their mission in Chile to a pair of young female students at the airport, and once they arrive on Easter Island, it becomes clear that there’s a more serious element to the trip.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Even after Chris reveals that he’s dying of cancer, “Wild Horse Nine” remains snappy and snarky, with some farcical phone calls between Lee and the pair’s CIA boss, MJ (Steve Buscemi), who’s concerned about Chris’ newfound tendency to share state secrets. Rockwell and Malkovich have excellent chemistry, like Colin Farrell and Brendan Gleeson as the hitmen in “In Bruges,” and it’s amusing to hear them talk so flippantly about all the people they’ve killed and governments they’ve toppled.

The coup in Chile has yet to happen by the time the movie ends, and aside from a few opening scenes and cutaways, the entire story takes place on Easter Island. So McDonagh’s examination of American imperialism is entirely theoretical, mostly limited to Chris’ warnings to the Chilean students, who wind up staying at the same hotel. It’s hard to take the political points seriously, since they’re tossed off in such a sarcastic way, but the overall portrayal of the CIA as backstabbing degenerates makes the message clear enough.

“Wild Horse Nine” is not as absurd as another Malkovich-starring movie about CIA agents leaking vital information, the Coen brothers’ “Burn After Reading,” and it gets a bit lost in its increasingly urgent plot points about the JFK assassination conspiracy. It also gets a tad sentimental about the relationship between Lee and Chris as Chris faces the end of his life, and McDonagh’s writing remains too mannered to produce any genuine empathy. But the actors do their best to sell the connection between the characters, and there are some quiet, contemplative moments toward the end.

Those are nice enough, but “Wild Horse Nine” could have used more of the manic energy that Parker Posey brings to her two brief scenes as Lee’s philandering wife, calling him from the U.S. McDonagh is much better at crafting witty repartee between two reprehensible characters than he is at generating heartfelt honesty.

It’s unlikely that “Wild Horse Nine” will generate the same awards attention given to McDonagh’s heavier films, but it’s a more pleasant viewing experience than a movie like “The Banshees of Inisherin.” Shot on location on Easter Island, it also functions as a gorgeous travelogue, with striking views of the iconic statuary. Malkovich, who’s been in far too many direct-to-video thrillers lately, gets a worthy showcase for his talents, and Rockwell generates laughs via his facial expressions alone.

I doubt I’ll ever become a McDonagh fan, but if he sticks with modest, entertaining movies like “Wild Horse Nine,” I’ll be more inclined to look forward to his next project.

“Wild Horse Nine” opens Nov. 6 in theaters

Josh Bell is a freelance writer and movie/TV critic based in Las Vegas. He's the former film editor of Las Vegas Weekly and has written about movies and TV for Vulture, Inverse, CBR, Crooked Marquee and more. With comedian Jason Harris, he co-hosts the podcast Awesome Movie Year.

Microchip (MCHP) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Industrial and data center demand pushed sales higher amid tight capacity.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Microchip Technology Incorporated (MCHP -13.92%) reported a shift in the business environment from broad customer inventory digestion to lengthening lead times and supply constraints across several product lines. Management reported that broad-based demand, particularly in the industrial and data center segments, drove a 9.2% sequential revenue increase. The company is managing a transition in its data center portfolio, where it is regaining market share with Gen 6 PCIe products after previously losing share in the Gen 5 generation. Management indicated that while AI infrastructure demand has been explosive, the company is preparing for a potential deceleration in that sector while focusing on its core industrial and automotive markets. Strategic focus has shifted toward integrating recent acquisitions and managing capacity constraints at outsourced semiconductor assembly and test (OSAT) partners, which have been impacted by high demand from the AI industry.

Operator: As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. I will now turn the conference over to Eric Bjornholt, Senior Vice President and CFO. Thank you, Eric. You may begin.

Eric Bjornholt: All right. Thank you, and good afternoon, everyone. During the course of this conference call, we will be making projections and other forward-looking statements regarding future events or the future financial performance of the company. We wish to caution you that such statements are predictions and that actual events or results may differ materially. We refer you to our press releases of today, as well as our recent filings with the SEC that identify important risk factors that may impact Microchip's business and results of operations. In attendance with me today are Steve Sanghi, Microchip's President and CEO, and Sajid Daudi, Microchip's Head of Investor Relations.

I will comment on our second quarter fiscal year 2027 financial performance, and Steve will provide commentary on our results and discuss the current business environment as well as our guidance. We will then be available to respond to specific investor and analyst questions. We are including information in our press release and on this conference call on various GAAP and non-GAAP measures. We have posted a full GAAP to non-GAAP reconciliation on the investor relations page of our website at www.microchip.com and included reconciliation information in our earnings press release, which we believe you will find useful when comparing our GAAP and non-GAAP results.

We have also posted a summary of our outstanding debt and leverage metrics on our website. I will now go through some of the operating results, including net sales, gross margin, and operating expenses. Other than net sales, I will be referring to these results on a non-GAAP basis, which is based on expenses prior to the effect of our acquisition activities, share-based compensation, and certain other adjustments as described in our earnings press release and in the reconciliations on our website. Our non-GAAP financial results met or exceeded the guidance we provided on August 6, 2026. Net sales in the September quarter were $1.622 billion, which was up 9.2% sequentially and up 42.2% from the September 2025 quarter.

We had several infrequent items that positively impacted net sales by about $53 million in the September quarter that we do not expect to repeat in the December quarter. These items were all at 100% gross margin and included approximately $21 million impact of our pricing change on inventory in the distribution channel, $22 million in licensing revenue from patent licensing transactions in excess of what we expect in the December quarter, and a $10 million benefit from a long-term contract with a customer. We have posted a summary of our net sales by product line and geography on our website for your reference. On a non-GAAP basis, gross margins were 66.5%, including capacity underutilization charges of $26.6 million.

Non-GAAP gross margins were positively impacted by higher licensing revenue in the quarter, as well as the impact of our pricing change that was implemented in the September quarter. Non-GAAP operating expenses were at 27% of sales, and operating income was 39.5% of sales. Non-GAAP net income was $559.2 million, and non-GAAP earnings per diluted share were $0.97, which was $0.04 above the midpoint of our guidance. On a GAAP basis in the September quarter, gross margins were 65.9%.

Total operating expenses were $603.5 million and included acquisition intangible amortization of $90.2 million, special charges of $7.9 million, which was primarily driven by our activities associated with our closure of Fab 2, and acquisition-related expenses, share-based compensation of $62.2 million, and $5.3 million of other expenses. The GAAP net income attributable to common shareholders was $373.6 million, or $0.68 per share, and included a tax benefit of $0.13 per share related to a valuation allowance release on tax attributes. Our non-GAAP cash tax rate was 5.7% in the September quarter, and we expect to have a tax rate of about 6.5% for fiscal year 2027.

Our tax rate is down from our previous forecast for a variety of reasons, including the impacts of a reduction in inventory reserves and a higher percentage of our taxable income being in jurisdictions with lower tax rates. Our inventory balance at September 30, 2026, was $1.117 billion. We had 184 days of inventory at the end of the September quarter, which was up nine days from the end of the June quarter. Inventory increased in the quarter as we received some high-dollar-value wafers from our foundry partners in anticipation of future growth and built some finished goods inventory.

Included in our September quarter ending inventory was 12 days of long life cycle, high-margin products whose manufacturing capacity has been end-of-lifed by our supply chain partners. Inventory at our distributors in the September quarter was at 25 days, which was flat compared to the June quarter and at the lower end of what we have experienced historically. Our cash flow from operating activities was $419.2 million in the September quarter. Our adjusted free cash flow was $377.1 million in the September quarter. As of September 30, our consolidated cash and total investment position was $457 million. Our net debt decreased by $31.7 million during the September quarter, and this is after funding the acquisition of Hailo.

Our adjusted EBITDA in the September quarter was $705.4 million and 43.5% of net sales. Our trailing 12-month adjusted EBITDA was $2.162 billion. Our net debt to adjusted EBITDA was 2.36 at September 30, 2026. We expect our net debt to adjusted EBITDA to continue to reduce as we progress through fiscal year 2027. Capital expenditures were $23.1 million in the September quarter, and we expect capital expenditures for fiscal year 2027 to be about $100 million. Depreciation expense in the September quarter was $36 million. I will now turn it over to Steve, who will provide some additional commentary on our September quarter results and our guidance for the December quarter. Steve?

Steve Sanghi: Thank you, Eric, and good afternoon, everyone. I will reflect a bit on our performance in the September quarter. The September quarter was an excellent quarter for Microchip, with broad-based growth as we beat the high end of our guidance in net sales, and our non-GAAP financial metrics like gross margin percentage, operating expense percentage, operating profit percentage, and earnings per share all met or beat our guidance. Our industrial and data center end markets were the two biggest drivers of our growth. Across our end markets, new design wins are increasingly supporting that growth.

Our end market split for the September quarter as a percentage of net sales, excluding licensing revenue, was industrial at 34.6%, data center at 16.7%, aerospace and defense at 16.1%, automotive at 14.2%, communication at 8.2%, consumer appliances at 7.3%, and compute at 2.9%. From the September quarter 2025 to the September quarter 2026, net sales from industrial grew 50.3%, data center grew 55.7%, aerospace and defense grew 41%, automotive grew 19.3%, communication grew 43.5%, consumer appliances grew 26.7%, and compute declined 1.6%. Net sales from our data center business unit was over $250 million in the September quarter and is well on its way to be $1 billion in calendar year 2026.

For calendar 2026, about half of our data center business comes from our standard products, and about half comes from our Data Center Solutions Business Unit. On the standard product side, design wins are building across many products. In power, our digital power controllers are winning across AI data center power applications, from server power supplies to battery backup units. In system management, we offer the parts that sense, control, and connect a server or rack, and our Gigabit Ethernet switches and USB hubs are gaining design win momentum in management networks.

With respect to our Gen 6 PCIe switch and retimer products, we had 19 design wins at the end of September quarter, up from 12 design wins at the end of June quarter. As of today, we now have 24 total design wins. These incremental wins will begin to ramp net sales next year and help position us across a growing range of next-generation data center platforms. As these deployments scale, so does our opportunity across the broader data center portfolio, including timing, security, and networking. I would like to give one more organizational update. As you know, after my return to Microchip as CEO two years ago, I reorganized the company into five pillars.

These pillars were microcontrollers, analog and power, networking and connectivity, high-performance compute, and AI on the edge. There is a Senior Vice President heading up each of these pillars. Our Data Center Solutions Business Unit reports into the networking and connectivity pillar. Because of our focus on our Data Center Solutions Business Unit, called the DCS, I have decided to pull up the DCS business unit to report directly to me at the executive staff level. The head of that business unit is Brian McCarson, who has spoken at these earnings calls before and will now report directly to me. Our design wins are not limited to data centers.

In aerospace and defense, we are winning designs across regions, products, and applications, from satellite communications and laser terminals to unmanned systems, by adapting our commercial technologies for space and defense. In one recent case, a defense technology company selected our radiation-tolerant-secure FPGA over its incumbent supplier for two space avionics programs. As defense production rates ramp, so does our opportunity. Security requirements are also growing across our markets, from data centers to defense to the edge. At the edge, a leading enterprise network provider recently selected our security solution to protect its wireless networks, where quantum-resistant encryption became a requirement.

Together, data center, aerospace and defense, industrial, robotics, automotive, and the intelligent edge are the secular trends we see driving growth over the long term. I will now talk about our business environment and the guidance going forward. Our bookings were very strong in the September quarter, and our book-to-bill ratio remained well above one. We started the December quarter with a higher backlog compared to when we entered the September quarter. The September quarter saw a pivot in the business environment, with customers completing their inventory digestion to shortages appearing in certain areas because of lengthening lead times at foundries, OSAT suppliers, and in our own fabs.

Lead times for the majority of our standard products historically have been four to eight weeks. But now, with our die inventory substantially reduced and foundry and OSAT capacity constraints, we are seeing lead times broadly stretch out on many of our products. Our customer requests for expedited shipments have increased significantly from a few quarters ago. We are receiving many orders for requests inside the lead time, and they are going unsupported in the quarter. We are advising our customers to give us longer-term backlog to help better match our new lead times, which vary a lot across our product lines. Our new lead times are anywhere from six weeks to 32 weeks.

Eric just explained three infrequent items in the last quarter. First, it was an unusually strong quarter in our licensing group, with a couple of large deals closing in the September quarter. Our licensing business is usually lumpy. The second item was the one-time effect of our recent price increases, which raised the value of our distribution inventory. The third was a revenue recognition from a long-term customer contract. Those infrequent items are not present in the December quarter. Our December quarter is also our weakest quarter seasonally, because there are a lot of holidays in the quarter. Therefore, the guidance for December quarter should be viewed in that context.

Our December quarter also includes the impact of two small acquisitions that we completed, Hailo in the AI on the edge space and VORAGO in the A&D space. We expect that Hailo will add about $4 million to our sales in the December quarter, and VORAGO will add about $6 million to our net sales in the December quarter. Taking all of these factors into account, we expect our net sales in the December quarter to be between down 3% and up 1%, with a midpoint of -1%. At the midpoint, this would be up 35.4% from the year-ago quarter. December quarter is usually seasonally down 3%-5%.

This seasonal effect, combined with the infrequent items we experienced in the September quarter, puts our December quarter guidance about 5%-7% above normal seasonality. We expect our non-GAAP gross margin to be between 64%-65% of net sales. This is because of several reasons. First, the three infrequent items in the September quarter were all at 100% gross margin. Secondly, our net sales guidance is lower than what we would have expected a quarter ago. Thirdly, we have substantial unsupported backlog on high-margin products that our supply chain cannot fulfill. And finally, we're running lower level of utilization internally than what we were planning a quarter ago. All these factors are impacting our gross margin guidance.

We expect our non-GAAP operating expenses to be between 26.5% and 27% of sales. We expect our non-GAAP operating profit to be between 37% and 38.5% of sales. We expect our non-GAAP diluted earnings per share to be between $0.87 and $0.95 per share. At the midpoint, non-GAAP earnings per share would be up 107% over the year-ago quarter. Regarding our long-term model, we maintain our long-term model on a non-GAAP basis to be 65% gross margin, 25% operating expense, and 40% operating profit. With that, operator, will you please call for questions?

Operator: Thank you. We will now be conducting a question-and-answer session. If you would like to ask a question, please press star one on your telephone keypad. A confirmation tone will indicate your line is in the question queue. You may press star two if you would like to remove your question from the queue. For participants using speaker equipment, it may be necessary to pick up your handset before pressing the star keys. So that we may address questions from as many participants as possible, we ask that you limit yourself to one question and one follow-up. If you have additional questions, you may requeue, and time permitting, those questions will be addressed.

One moment, please, while we poll for questions. Thank you. Our first question comes from the line of Timothy Arcuri with UBS. Please proceed.

Timothy Arcuri: Thanks a lot. Steve, I was wondering if you could help unpack a lot of these puts and takes. It seems like you said that December revenue is a little lower than what you would have thought three months ago, and fab utilization's also a little lower, yet bookings are stronger. Is this due to constraints that are downstream from you? Maybe, does that give you more visibility into March, so you could maybe give us a sense, maybe, normal seasonal, I think in March is up like 3%. Sounds like maybe you feel a little bit better about March as a result of all this. If you can just unpack that for us, that'd be great. Thanks.

Steve Sanghi: Yeah. So I think if you look at the last quarter, it was kind of noisy. The three items that we talked about were in our guidance. The licensing deals were in our guidance, the recognition of $10 million of customer contract was in our guidance, and so was the price increase. So the upside last quarter came from product sales, so we did very well. Now, when you look at the current quarter, lead times have stretched out, and a lot of the high-margin bookings we received on a lot of advanced products in pretty much every business segment cannot be fulfilled in this quarter. So, some of them are scheduled for next quarter.

As a result, I think we're guiding the December quarter to be a tad lower than we would have thought about a quarter ago. The backlog for March quarter today is much better than December quarter was at the same point in time. So, currently we're optimistic. We're not giving any guidance for the March quarter, but I think some of the constraints we're talking about are now scheduled into March quarter. So, we are hopeful that March quarter will be a stronger quarter.

Timothy Arcuri: Thanks a lot. Then maybe just as a quick follow-up. So, on the PCIe Gen 6 wins, I think, Steve, you said 14 design wins coming out of June, and it's now up to 24. Sounds like you're building some high-value inventory for that stuff too. So, when does that start to become really big revenue? If you can kind of size what the incremental could be from those wins, if possible, that'd be great.

Steve Sanghi: We are unable to dollarize the wins. The part went into production last quarter, and we have small amount of shipments in the current quarter, and they will increase every quarter from here on. Actually, there is sizable shipment this quarter, and they start increasing then from the next quarter on. The design wins are very strong, and they are turning on at various times next year. By the start of 2028, you can expect almost all 24 designs to be in production, and we are winning essentially a design a week almost.

Eric Bjornholt: Yeah, just one clarification to the way Tim asked that question is he said PCIe Gen 6, and the 24 design wins is both PCIe and the retimers. Just wanted to clarify that.

Steve Sanghi: That is correct. I think we are basically keeping them together. They are very much in the same application. Many times, retimer goes with the PCIe. We don't always win them both. In some cases, customer doesn't need the retimer, they only need the PCIe switch. But they are pretty much products are in sync. In the past, we have broken out how many on PCIe Gen 6 and how many on retimer, and I don't have that today, but we have no problem in breaking that out.

Timothy Arcuri: Okay, Steve. Thank you very much.

Operator: Thank you. Our next question comes from the line of Matthew Prisco with Cantor Fitzgerald. Please proceed.

Matthew Prisco: Hey, guys. Thanks for taking the question. I guess to start, if you could maybe discuss order linearity in the quarter and maybe what you've seen through October and November thus far, and any color you can offer on those booking trends across end markets would be appreciated. Thanks.

Eric Bjornholt: Bookings were quite strong all the way through September, so nothing really to point out there. And then October was another good month of bookings where backlog, overall backlog continued to grow. November's just been a few days, but nothing odd to report.

Steve Sanghi: Yeah. Bookings have continued well. The backlog has continued to build. The best way to look at our December quarter is to take the $53 million out of the September quarter, which were the three infrequent items. And if you do that, then our December quarter is up about 2.3% from our September quarter, versus normal seasonality for December quarter is down 3%-5%. Therefore, I think we got no issues in how the quarter is, how the product chip backlog is, how the bookings are. All that continues well.

But the two licensing deals, a unique customer program that added $10 million of revenue recognition last quarter, and this strange effect of when you ship the inventory to distributor, you assume there's a certain amount of disti price out protection on that, and then the rest of it you recognize as a revenue. When you increase the price, then that disti price protection becomes less, and you have to capture that as a one-time revenue in the quarter. That was about $21 million. Those items made it very noisy. If you peel through all that, and we're giving you complete visibility, if you take those out, then the quarter is up 2.3%.

In that 2.3%, there's $10 million from two acquisitions, $4 million from Hailo and $6 million from VORAGO. We broke that out too, so take that out too if you like, and the 2.3% will go down to maybe 1.5% or something, versus down 4% at the midpoint. There's no problem in how the business is doing, and we remain optimistic for the next quarter. Backlog is good, bookings are coming in. That's how you should view the quarter in that context.

Matthew Prisco: Very helpful. Thank you. Maybe you can offer some more color on what you're seeing in terms of those constraints today, how those foundry OSAT constraints changed over the last 90 days. I believe you added internal constraints this quarter. How are you responding to these constraints overall, and how do you expect that situation to evolve as we look into 2027? Thank you.

Steve Sanghi: Incrementally, we have additional capacity allocation every quarter, which will continue to provide growth. But we could use more than that. On various different nodes, we have more capacity the following quarter. Therefore, some of the orders from this quarter are scheduled into next quarter, and I believe some of the orders from the next quarter will move into the June quarter, where we have more capacity. We're not unique. I think the entire industry is seeing it. Demand is stronger than the capacity available. With whatever is happening in the market right now with data center, AI infrastructure, concerns whether they will all be built or not built, I think there's a silver lining in it.

The whole thing was just too much hot air, and if some air comes out and capacity becomes available at OSATs and foundries for broad-based business, I think there is still silver lining in it.

Operator: Thank you. Our next question comes from the line of Joe Quatrochi with Wells Fargo. Please proceed.

Joe Quatrochi: Yeah, thanks for taking the question. You saw a nice acceleration in the industrial demand this quarter. I was wondering if you could just talk about what you're seeing there, and maybe just on that last line you said, how do we think about your exposure to just everything around the data center infrastructure build, maybe outside of the data hall?

Steve Sanghi: I think, when it comes to industrial is the broadest market segment there is. I mean, what is industrial? It's not a narrow set of things. Industrial is factories, cities, hotels, lighting, robotics, medical. Industrial is very, very broad, and we're seeing opportunities in industrial. Some of it got driven by industrial was sort of the last one to recover, I think, two or three quarters ago. So, it's seeing substantial recovery from that point, and a lot of new design wins in every single area, robotics, medical, process control, factories, farming, equipment, appliances. Everything is growing, so it's very, very difficult to kind of explain industrial beyond that because it's so broad.

The second point of your question is on the data centers. We are early in the data center penetration, because we didn't have a lot of Gen 5 switch revenue, and you've known from past that we were very late to market on it, and therefore, we lost the Gen 5 business. With Gen 6, we are regaining a lot of the market share, and a lot of it is not in our revenue. So, for us, it's all upside, where if it slows down, it could be downside for the people who have a lot of that business.

In terms of our other half of our data center exposure, which is through microcontroller, analog, power, switches, security, USB, Ethernet, these are very broad-based products where data center is really a small portion of the overall shipments to all the end markets. If certain data centers won't get built, a lot of that upside is not in our forecast.

Joe Quatrochi: Thanks. As we're thinking about just kind of the gross margin puts and takes into the December quarter, how should we think about this underutilization charges? There's a nice improvement this quarter. Do we keep thinking that's somewhat linear as we move forward?

Eric Bjornholt: It will not be linear. This quarter, we are not anticipating any sort of big improvement. We actually built finished goods last quarter and don't really want to do that again this quarter, so we're slowing down some of the assembly and test operations. We're still doing the ramp in our wafer fabs, where Steve had mentioned in his prepared remarks that die inventory is getting low in certain cases. It's a balance, but you will not see the large reduction in utilization or underutilization charges this quarter that you saw last quarter. That's still upside in front of us in future quarters.

Steve Sanghi: December quarter historically is kind of weak, so we took that in cautiously, but it will accelerate again next quarter because then we go into good March quarter followed by two strong June and September quarters before we get another weak December quarter next year. So utilization should start to improve, but not linearly.

Steve Sanghi: Well, Vivek, I think there is data center and non-data center. There is nothing to worry about in the non-data center. Industrial is doing fine. Automotive is continuing to recover. Aerospace and defense is doing well. Industrial is doing well. Automotive is doing well. Communication is doing well. Steve Sanghi: Yeah, I think yes. Like I said, there is data center and non-data center. The non-data center demand is normal. It is doing well. Data center demand has been explosive. If that comes down, its effect on us would be very small because we are very much in the early stage. We are not doing billions of dollars of revenue in the data center, and all of our rest of the markets are growing, and data center is 16.7% of our business, not 100% like many of the other companies. Where the crowding out is not in demand. The crowding out is in capacity.

Data center has been crowding out capacity for trailing edge nodes because of power requirements and analog requirements and all these other things, microcontrollers and others. So, data center has been crowding out capacity at the OSAT, and in many cases, we have lost capacity, where some of it we had to bring it inside, some of it we had to move it elsewhere, and it takes time to requalify. Therefore, the backlog could not be served and will be served some in the next quarter, some in the quarter after. I think a deceleration on the data center will probably have a positive effect on our business because some of that capacity frees up.

So we're making those moves, but those moves take six months. Some of this displacement only happened just a couple of months ago. So those are the things we are struggling with, and that's why I said that some deceleration of the AI demand actually would be quite positive early on.

Steve Sanghi: Our in-house microcontroller and microprocessor product lines did not have AI acceleration in them. No hardware acceleration. We had some software acceleration. We did have some hardware acceleration available on our FPGAs and our Data Center Solutions Business Unit, but not on our microcontroller business units and the microprocessor business unit, we did not. When we bought Hailo, in our microprocessor business unit, we were developing products. The first of those products was going to be available in about two and a half, three years, and the second of them was going to be available in about four and a half years, because they were serial in nature.

The product line that Hailo brought to us accelerates the roadmap by five years. Therefore, we have canceled the two products that we were developing in the NPU business unit because they have been replaced by Hailo. In fact, I have merged the Hailo and NPU business unit together, and we are advancing the Hailo roadmap, and we have canceled our NPU roadmap in the microprocessor business unit. That's how important Hailo has been for us.

Joshua Buchalter: Hey, guys. Thanks for taking my question. Steve, you mentioned sort of a reluctance to raise pricing so close to the previous increase. Steve Sanghi: I think we were very careful in raising prices. We immediately didn't jump on it back in January, February. Steve Sanghi: I will go back to data center and non-data center. I do not see any issues in non-data center. Christopher Rolland: Understood. Given the constraints that you guys have in the expanding lead times, it does seem like this is from external manufacturing as opposed to internal. Steve Sanghi: I mean, we are an IDM, integrated device manufacturer. Our bias always is what we can produce inside, we produce inside. Let's take package first, and I'll take the fab. We largely do packages where we do not have enough volume to do it cost-effectively inside. In some cases, our volume are such we can't even keep one set of tools, one pilot line busy. If you can't, then your utilization will be very low and the cost will be higher. So those are the ones we do outside. Never had an issue with it.

This is sort of the first time where AI is crowding it out and having us think through even bringing some things inside, which may be slightly higher cost, but at least we'll have the capacity. Now, if you come on the fab side, when we were considering a 300 mm fab during the post-COVID years, our plan was to do it under a license with a major foundry. Because all our wafers ran in that foundry, and the foundry was saying they will not add any trailing edge capacity, therefore, they were agreeing to give us a license at 300 mm to bring those processes inside.

When the COVID cycle busted and they had enough capacity, then we didn't want to spend a couple of billion dollars or more of CapEx, nor did they want to give the license, so that program went away. Today, the problems are not as big, and they're not as severe, and they're not multi-year problems where we have to go back and build a few billion dollars of capital to build a 300 mm fab and seek a license again. We're not thinking that because we don't think the problem is that long. It's just a short-term issue.

Eric Bjornholt: I just want to add one piece of clarification to the way you asked the question. We do have capacity constraints with some of our internal production also. There are certain products where we are behind, we cannot meet customers' lead times, and we are working as hard as we can to bring on capacity. As Steve mentioned before, even when we bring on that capacity, sometimes if it is outsourced for assembly test, we have capacity constraints even when it comes out of our fab. These constraints have grown beyond just being an outside foundry issue for us.

Operator: Thank you. Our last question comes from the line of Tore Svanberg with Stifel. Please proceed.

Tore Svanberg: Yes, thank you. Steve, I had a question on the OSAT capacity, the external capacity that is impacting your front-end capacity. How much visibility do you have there? Steve Sanghi: There are multiple answers to that question. In some cases, our allocation at that OSAT is not increasing, so we are qualifying a different OSAT in a different country, and some of that is scheduled to be in production in the middle of next quarter. We will get some incremental for half the quarter and then have more even in the June quarter. In some other cases, the current OSAT is asking us to load it with a minimum batch size. Because when the batch sizes are smaller, it takes more capacity, it is less efficient for the manufacturer. So they are asking us to do minimum batch sizes.

Our volume on those packages is small, and we cannot meet the minimum batch size. Therefore, either you pay the extra price for breaking the batch size, or you load it infrequently, once every two weeks, so you can bundle up and meet the minimum batch size, which again then pushes some revenue outside the quarter because you cannot meet the batch size. In some other cases, we are tooling up that capacity inside, and several different programs, and some are in production next quarter, some will even take a quarter longer. It is all over the place. But more capacity becomes available next quarter, June quarter, and from there on.

Steve Sanghi: Yeah. So 24 design wins will go to production, but its volume could be slightly lower than if the bubble were to continue. We do not always know the customer who we have design wins with, when they go to production, are they shipping into a data center which already exists, or they are shipping into a data center in Ohio that the local governor is going to put on hold. We do not know that, right? It is all over the place. But it is much smaller portion of our business, so the impact on us from that slowdown would be a fraction of what it would be compared to somebody who is 100% business is in data center.

Tore Svanberg: Yeah. That's fair. Thank you very much.

Steve Sanghi: The fact that on many of the products, like the Gen 6 switch and retimer, it's largely all upside for us because we have no business in it. The other comment I want to make is, if you conservatively model fiscal year 2028, that begins on April 1, with whatever assumptions you have, even in the extreme conservative case, I come up with over $4 of earnings. The stock is at 15x earnings.

Steve Sanghi: I tell you, I'm tempted to buy it myself. [I might].

Operator: Thank you. That concludes today's question and answer session. I would like to turn the floor back over to Mr. Sanghi for closing comments.

Steve Sanghi: Thank you for attending our conference call. I think next conference we go to will be UBS, right?

Eric Bjornholt: Sanghi is at a conference next week. But yeah, we'll have a large Microchip presence at UBS with a larger group of executives.

Steve Sanghi: We'll see some of you there. Thank you very much.

Operator: This concludes today's teleconference. You may disconnect your lines at this time. Thank you for your participation.
