Simon (SPG) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Leasing momentum and rising rents lifted results despite higher interest costs.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Simon Property Group, Inc. (SPG -0.85%) reported growth across its retail platforms driven by leasing velocity and record sales productivity. Management stated that traffic acceleration and broad-based tenant demand supported a 17% increase in base rent for new deals while simultaneously reducing capital investment per transaction. The company entered into an agreement to acquire University Village in Seattle and announced plans to break ground on a new Nashville development, Sagefield, on Nov. 12, 2026. Management remains focused on its $1.2 billion redevelopment pipeline and opportunistic share repurchases, utilizing free cash flow to fund investment activity while maintaining a net debt to EBITDA ratio of five times.

Operator: Please note this event is being recorded. I would now like to turn the conference over to Mr. Tom Ward, Senior Vice President of Investor Relations. Please go ahead, sir.

Tom Ward: Thank you, Chuck, and thank you for joining us this evening. Presenting on today's call are Eli Simon, Chief Executive Officer, President, and Chief Operating Officer, and Brian McDade, Chief Financial Officer. A quick reminder that statements made during this call may be deemed forward-looking statements within the meaning of the Safe Harbor of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995, and actual results may differ materially due to a variety of risks, uncertainties, and other factors. We refer you to today's press release and our SEC filings for a detailed discussion of the risk factors relating to those forward-looking statements. Please note that this call includes information that may be accurate only as of today's date.

Reconciliations of non-GAAP financial measures to the most directly comparable GAAP measures are included within the press release and the supplemental information in today's Form 8-K filing. Both the press release and the supplemental information are available on our IR website at investors.simon.com. Our conference call this evening will be limited to one hour. For those who would like to participate in the question-answer-session, we ask you please limit yourself to one question. Please introduce Eli Simon.

Eli Simon: Good evening. We delivered another excellent quarter of financial and operational results. Domestic property NOI grew 6.6%, and real estate FFO per share grew 4.7% compared to the prior year period. Our results reflect continued operating momentum across all of our platforms, healthy leasing fundamentals, and the added contribution of our acquisitions last year. Traffic growth accelerated in the third quarter, furthering a trend we have seen throughout 2026. Sales productivity is at record levels for each of our platforms, underscoring the health of the retailers in our centers and the quality of our real estate. Our portfolio is well-located in growing markets, and our ongoing investment keeps our centers the destination of choice for shoppers and retailers alike.

Last week, we announced that we entered into an agreement to acquire University Village, a premier open-air retail destination in Seattle, one of the most attractive markets in the country. This acquisition reflects our focus on owning the highest quality assets, and the center's exceptional mix of national brands, local boutiques and restaurants make it a natural fit for our portfolio. We see real opportunity to build on the center's nearly 70 years of success through leasing, operations and future expansion. University Village expands our local presence alongside our recently rebranded. The District at Northgate, where we opened our first multifamily property with additional retail this summer.

We look forward to serving the Seattle community, retailers and shoppers at both of these assets in the many years to come. The transaction is expected to close in the fourth quarter, subject to customary closing conditions. Moving on to leasing. Tenant demand remains widespread, from established national and international brands to emerging fast-growing concepts. During the third quarter, we signed nearly 1,200 leases totaling over 4.3 million sq ft. New deals represented approximately 25% of total sq ft leased. Year-to-date through the third quarter, initial base minimum rent per sq ft on new deals is up 17% year-over-year, while tenant allowance per sq ft is down 10%.

In other words, we are achieving higher rents while investing less capital per deal. Our pipeline of prospective deals is well ahead of where it was a year ago at this time. Under retailer sales, the malls and Premium Outlets were at $852 per sq ft, up 14.8% year-over-year. Importantly, total sales volume increased 6.8% in the quarter and 7.1% over the trailing 12 months. Comparable sales grew 4.9% in the third quarter and 5% over the trailing 12 months. Onto our development and redevelopment activity, where at the end of the quarter, we had development projects underway across all platforms, with our share of the net cost totaling $1.2 billion at a blended yield of 9%.

We continue to be very excited about the projects under development, as well as our pipeline, given the strong tenant demand from best-in-class retailers and restaurateurs we have for these new developments and redevelopments. Separately, next Thursday, we will break ground on Sagefield, our new ground-up development south of Nashville, which we expect to open by the end of 2028. It will be a truly special center with over 80 retail shops and an impressive lineup of restaurants across 100 acres with a number of community gathering spaces. More to come on this exciting development.

Finally, in September, we launched our new brand campaign, "It's a Simon Thing." For the first time, this campaign brings all of our centers together under the Simon name, highlighting what makes a visit to our centers different. Customer response has been tremendous. This is already the most engaged campaign in our company's history, and we expect that enthusiasm to build throughout the holiday season, which is good for our tenants. With that, I'll turn it over to Brian, who will review our financial results from the third quarter in more detail and provide an update on our outlook for the remainder of the year.

Brian McDade: Thank you, Eli. Real estate FFO was $1.28 billion, or $3.37 per share in the third quarter compared to $1.22 billion, or $3.22 per share in the prior year period, an increase of 4.7% per share. Higher lease income, including overage, along with disciplined cost management and our acquisitions last year drove a $0.27 contribution from our domestic and international operations. As anticipated, higher net interest expense reduced FFO by $0.05 year-over-year. Reported FFO was $3.09 per share in the third quarter compared to $3.25 per share in the prior year period.

This third quarter includes $0.27 per share of non-cash after-tax losses from unrealized mark-to-market adjustments to the Klépierre exchangeable bond and the revaluation of an equity security that became publicly traded during the quarter and is held in one of our unconsolidated other platform investments. Domestic property NOI increased 6.6% year-over-year for the quarter and 7.3% for the nine months. Approximately 120 basis points of growth for both the third quarter and nine months was attributable to our acquisition of the remaining TRG interest. Portfolio NOI, which includes our international properties at constant currency, grew 6.7% for the quarter and 7.2% for the nine months.

Malls and Premium Outlets occupancy at the end of the third quarter was 96.5%, a 50 basis point increase sequentially and a 10 basis point increase year-over-year, driven by our strong leasing activity. This ties our highest combined occupancy level since 2016. The Mills occupancy was 98.9%. Average base minimum rent for the malls and Premium Outlets increased 6.1% year-over-year, while ABR for The Mills increased 11%. Occupancy cost at the end of the quarter was 12.4%. Turning to the balance sheet. During the quarter, we were active in the debt markets by issuing $800 million of U.S. senior notes in two tranches, with a combined average term of 7.7 years and a weighted average rate of 5.45%.

We also completed seven secured loan transactions totaling approximately $970 million at a weighted average interest rate of 6.33%. Through the first nine months of the year, we closed 25 secured transactions totaling $4.67 billion at a 5.3% average rate. We ended the quarter with approximately $9 billion in liquidity. The momentum of our results, together with the improvement in our capital efficiency, has increased our free cash flow generation by more than 7.5% year to date. That, combined with our natural deleveraging balance sheet, gives us a real competitive advantage.

With net debt to EBITDA of five times and a fixed charge coverage ratio of 4.6 times, we have the flexibility to continue to invest in our portfolio to make our assets even better. Turning to dividend and share repurchases. Today, we announced our dividend of $2.30 per share for the fourth quarter, an increase of $0.10 or 4.5% year-over-year. The dividend is payable on December 31st to shareholders as of the record date. Importantly, during the quarter, we repurchased 1.1 million shares of our common stock. In year to date, we have repurchased 3.4 million shares of common stock and limited partnership units for an investment of $667 million at an average purchase price of $198.97 per share.

Finally, onto 2026 guidance. Given our results for the first nine months and our current view for the remainder of the year, we are raising our full year 2026 real estate FFO guidance to $13.30-$13.40 per share, a $0.10 increase compared to the range previously provided. At the midpoint, the new range represents growth of approximately 5% as compared to our $12.73 of real estate FFO last year. Thank you. We are now available for your questions.

Operator: Thank you. We will now begin the question-and-answer session. To ask a question, you may press star then one on your touch-tone phone. If you are using a speakerphone, please pick up your handset before pressing the keys. If at any time your question has been addressed and you would like to withdraw your question, please press star then two. Our first question for today will come from Michael Goldsmith with UBS. Please go ahead.

Michael Goldsmith: Good afternoon. Thanks a lot for taking my question. Eli, on University Village, can you give us a sense of the going-in yield and what you think the stabilized yield could be after the leasing, operating, and expansion opportunities you mentioned? Maybe more broadly, does the acquisition change at all how you think about open-air versus enclosed malls in terms of tenant demand, growth, and where you want to deploy capital? Thanks.

Eli Simon: Sure. Thanks for the question. On the first, unfortunately, one is we obviously haven't closed yet, but two is, based on the agreement, I can't provide any detail on pricing. What I can say is that the transaction will be modestly accretive next year, NAV accretive day one, and we're very excited. We think this will grow well above our average portfolio growth for the many years to come, given the quality of the asset and the market. I do not think of this asset as an open-air center versus another asset as an enclosed center. That's really not how we run our business.

Stanford's open air, Roosevelt Field enclosed, and we lease them the same, which is to make sure we have the best tenants for that asset to serve the customers in that market. That's really how we operate. On further acquisitions, we look if there is opportunity to buy really, really good retail real estate in well-located markets, where we think if we can operate it and operate it, lease it, run it, potentially expand it, that we can add value, and it is at a fair price, we will do it. We never had one conversation internally where we said, "This is outdoor versus indoor." That is just not the way we think about our portfolio.

We think about our portfolio today and the potential growth of the portfolio as we want to own really good retail real estate where tenants want to be and where customers want to shop.

Operator: Your next question will come from Samir Khanal with Bank of America. Please go ahead.

Samir Khanal: Good afternoon, everybody. I guess, Eli, there is clearly a lot of good momentum here on leasing. Given the healthy organic growth across the portfolio that we are seeing, how should we think about sort of the building blocks to growth next year, the bottom line, right? As you balance this continued leasing momentum, this operating momentum you are seeing against the headwinds from higher refinancing costs and any other offsets we need to consider. Thanks.

Eli Simon: Thanks for the question. We are obviously in the midst of our budgeting process now. I think we spent literally 24 hours the last two weeks going through property by property, which is exciting, because I think what is exciting is that we are seeing strong demand and depth of demand across the portfolio, not concentrated in any given geography or any product type or any type of tenant, but really across the portfolio. Obviously, that is the key driver. The key driver of growth obviously is leasing and leasing velocity.

That said, there are other factors, including sales, which is, until we kind of see how the holiday season plays out, it is really hard to have a great view on 2027 sales until the biggest months in 2026 are complete. On interest expense, it is no secret, and we have said this, I think, last quarter, it will probably be in that similar $0.30-ish range of headwinds next year. Obviously, this is volatile right now, so we do not have a great number either, but it is going to be something like that.

We feel confident in the portfolio, confident in our ability to execute, and we will obviously have more about guidance in February of next year when we report our fourth quarter earnings. When we look at the business today, we really do remain excited about the opportunities. Again, it is not just the leasing, it is the development, it is our Simon Brand Ventures business. It is really across the board. Obviously, it is on us to execute, but we will give you more detail on that early next year.

Operator: Your next question will come from Caitlin Burrows with Goldman Sachs. Please go ahead.

Caitlin Burrows: Hi, everyone. Maybe just on the acquisition side, obviously you did the deal in Seattle recently and have been somewhat active over the past year or two. As you go forward and look at the different opportunities that are out there, can you discuss, and I know you have talked about before, the different criteria for what makes something attractive to Simon, but to what extent you might be interested in a portfolio and say there is X amount of properties in the portfolio?

Would you say that each one would have to kind of stand on its own with those criteria, or might you consider a portfolio where you would kind of go for the whole thing even if some were not that great on their own?

Eli Simon: Thanks, Caitlin. I would say we are not going to buy for buying's sake. To me, buying a portfolio to get a few good things but take some things that you might not want, to me, that is buying for buying's sake, and that is really not in our plan right now. Our plan, to the extent we are active in the acquisition market, which, as you said, we have been in the last couple years. Obviously, we bought Brickell last year, which has been a phenomenal story for us. We bought Phillips Place, which will have some exciting news next year on building a multifamily building there with additional retail.

We bought The Mall, two luxury outlets in Italy, where we are going to be expanding the one in Florence next year. If you look at those transactions and obviously look at University Village, those are great assets that we want to own, that we want to own them forever. Buying a portfolio to get one or two of those assets but maybe take four, five, six of the assets that we don't feel that way, we feel good enough about the ability to grow internally with our redevelopment program, buy back stock, release, remerchandise our existing assets, and we'll run the business that way.

When you look at the criteria, again, really good, well-located real estate where tenants want to be, where shoppers want to be, where we think our platform can add value to it at a fair price. If we can do that, we'll do transactions. If not, we have more than enough to do with our existing portfolio. Which obviously has been very active on the development and redevelopment side.

Operator: The next question will come from Michael Griffin with Evercore ISI. Please go ahead.

Michael Griffin: Great, thanks. Eli, I'm curious if you can expand a bit on customer demand and sales patterns there. I mean, it seems like traffic continues to increase. Has this resulted in larger average ticket sizes, greater purchases? It seems that the demand is pretty broad-based, but I'm curious if there are any categories or price points that maybe are performing a little worse than you might have expected, or does it seem like it's full steam ahead as we head into the holidays? Thank you.

Eli Simon: It's a great question. I would say generally speaking, that it is very broad-based. It is across platforms, across geographies, across categories that we have seen growth. That growth is, again, 4.9%. It's obviously impressive for the quarter, but I think what's more impressive is, if you recall in August, I said this is where we started to hit tougher comps last year. Our two-year stack, that's 7.2% growth, which is actually 80 basis points above the two-year stack in Q2. Really, growth is accelerating from a sales perspective. It's the same story that we talked about in the second quarter athleisure, juniors brands, luxury, watches, jewelry.

One thing that is a nice story is we had actually the best quarter in three years in the restaurants in the third quarter, which that business has obviously been sort of flat-ish, I guess I would say, but we actually had a really nice growth story there. What we've seen is traffic is up and sales are up, and so it's hard for me to really say that people are buying more per person, people are buying bigger baskets, but I would say they are. Dwell time is up, they're going to more stores, and I think part of the reason they're going to more stores is we're adding more relevant stores at these centers.

We're adding great retailers, expanding national retailers that have been in our malls for a long time, complementing them with new brands that might be coming from overseas or coming from online, and are getting into our mall portfolio. We really haven't seen any slowdown. October traffic in line, just got this two hours ago, in line with the third quarter in sort of that mid 3% growth range. What we're hearing from the retailers is optimism for the holiday season, which is, as you know, is our Super Bowl, our time to shine. We're focused on getting these stores open and continue to add these new relevant retailers to our portfolio.

Operator: The next question will come from Vince Tibone with Green Street Advisors. Please go ahead.

Vince Tibone: Hi, good evening. I wanted to ask about how we should think about the buyback strategy going forward. I believe you have about $2 million shares left to repurchase to effectively offset the new shares issued as part of the Taubman deal. What I am really trying to get at is, after presumably you do a little bit more buyback related to the Taubman, do you see share repurchases being a big part of the capital allocation toolkit in the coming years? Or was the buybacks really just the desire to offset the Taubman deal, and once those shares are done, it is going to be more focused on redevelopment or other acquisitions or other capital uses?

Eli Simon: First, Vince, it is actually about 450,000 shares left to buy back that $5 million and change shares we issued for Taubman. The way I think about it is, we love owning our assets, and if we can own more of them at a price that is attractive, we are going to do it. The reality is, we generate a significant amount of free cash flow after dividend and after capital. We are naturally deleveraging. We are effectively sub 5x now. We see the embedded growth of that $1.2 billion development pipeline that is in the ground today, but we know there is the $4 billion of shadow pipeline plus behind it.

I look at it and say, we are generating the free cash flow. It is a very good use of free cash flow. It does not preclude us from doing acquisitions. It does not preclude us from doing developments and redevelopments. Do I think that we will continue a steady cadence on the buyback? I do. Obviously, we are going to evaluate market conditions. We are going to be thoughtful on it and not just do it to do it. But if we can do it accretively and do it with excess free cash, that is pretty interesting. I mean, if you think about it, the business is growing round numbers, $200 million or so a year.

To stay at 5x, that is $1 billion of leverage. I mean, that is pretty powerful. Again, not saying we are going to add leverage to buy back stock, but this business is a powerful machine, and I think one of the uses of the free cash flow that we are generating should be to buy back our stock, because I look at it and say, "I would love to own more Sawgrass." Right? Going through that leasing plan in detail yesterday, it is incredible. If we can own more of it, let us own more of it.

We are not going to have any specific guidance around it besides, obviously, as you said, we will finish buying back the Taubman stake. But we will be thoughtful about it, and as Brian said, we bought back at $199 per share this year, which is, I think, pretty disciplined. We will see, but it would not surprise me if we continue to do more even after we buy back that remaining piece.

Operator: Your next question will come from Craig Mailman with Citi. Please go ahead.

Craig Mailman: Hey, guys. I'm just kind of curious. I know, Eli, you had said that the rate environment is fluctuating and there's not a lot of visibility, which feels like been the case for a while here. As you guys think about kind of the cost of capital or your normalized cost of capital to go or no-go on projects, I mean, you just spent time talking about the amount of free cash flow that you're throwing off and what leverage does if you kind of don't issue every year.

I'm just kind of curious how disconnected your cost of capital is from the rate environment today, given how much free cash flow you throw off and how we should think about kind of that tie to the rate environment versus maybe some other companies that need to hit the capital markets to fund new investments.

Eli Simon: It's a great question. I'd say, the cost of capital is still connected to the rate environment, I mean, just definitionally, I guess. When I think about our development pipeline or our development projects, and we had our capital committee meeting on Monday, a long meeting, which is good because there's a lot of projects. When I look at that, the first thing I look at every single time is there tenant demand? That's sort of the overarching principle. If there's no tenant demand, I don't really care where rates are and what hypothetical spread we can build to. We're just not going to do it. First and foremost is, do we have tenant demand? And the answer is yes.

When I look at building at our $1.2 billion pipeline, which is really $1.4 billion if you fast-forward to today, right? We added approved projects on Monday. That's a, I would say, 300 basis point spread, more, I guess, now with rates as they've moved in the past week or so.

Over a long time period, which is how we think about these developments, we're not merchant builders that are looking to flip, which you got to time it exactly right and say, "Okay, my 300 basis point spread, when I deliver it, and when I stabilize it, I'm going to hit X spread so I can make my 20% IRR." We look at it and say, "If I'm building to a 9% return and I'm going to own this forever, the cost of capital is important, of course, but that's a very healthy spread." A very healthy spread. Again, it goes back to the tenant demand.

The projects that we're talking about, it's not just names on a page, it's signed leases. So we're really building with signed leases, with signed LOIs, and with depth behind those. The other piece that I think is important is that these returns are only on the capital we are investing. If you look at a Brea, look at a Southdale, look at a Briarwood, those returns were great, but we've added at Southdale, I think it's something like 80 new tenants because of the transformational project we did. Brea, which is still not even fully opened, I think it fully opens in the next week or two with all the restaurants.

We're going to change that whole inside of the mall, add capital for renovation, and then continue to add new tenants, and those tenants are comping up 10%, 12%. Those are the tenants that have already been here. The new tenants, they're going to be comping up not even till this time next year. So it's a good position to be in. I think, yeah, the fact that we are building this from free cash flow, all cash, is 100% a competitive advantage. When the world turns, if we have the tenant demand and we like the math, we can still do it when others can't, which makes it even more valuable real estate when we're done.

Again, as rates are high, we're incredibly disciplined on what we do. If they go down, we're incredibly disciplined on what we do. We look at every dollar, we look at every tenant, we look at every basis point. But where we are today, we have not changed really our viewpoint on this development pipeline because rates went up, I don't know, 50, 60 basis points from where they were this summer.

Operator: The next question will come from Haendel St. Juste with Mizuho. Please go ahead.

Ravi Vaidya: Hi there. Good evening. This is Ravi Vaidya on the line for Haendel. Hope you guys are doing well. How should we think about internal growth going forward at this point? Occupancy is at 96.5%. It seems it's near its all-time highs, and with maybe further upside seeming a bit light. Can you offer us color on the in-place rent bumps, and how does that compare to the leases you're assigning? Thanks.

Eli Simon: I mean, the internal growth, obviously, again, I don't know if you consider it internal or not, is obviously the development and redevelopment is a big part of it, which I just talked about, the $1.2 billion, which is really a $1.4 billion, with the $4 billion pipeline behind it. That's earning a 9% return, so that's $450 million of incremental EBITDA. That's pretty good. That's a big part of it. But if you exclude that, our job is to continue to re-merchandise these centers, and that's not a, we get to 100% and we stop, okay? Our foot's on the gas always.

We don't say, "Okay, we only have X number of leases to sign and then we're done." That's not how we operate. We say, "Okay, how can we be better?" If you go to SouthPark, if you go to Boca, these centers have had incredible growth coming out of COVID the last four or five years. When we went through our budgets meetings last week, it wasn't, "Okay, we did a great job," which we did, and the team did a great job. It's what's next. What's next is continuing to re-merchandise, add more productive tenants. If you look at it, we're signing new deals today at $78 a foot, and last year it was at $65 a foot.

As tenants become more productive, and we can bring in more productive tenants to the center, they can pay more rent, and NOI grows. Yes, we get rent bumps, yes, we get renewals that are in, call it, the mid-single digit return, mid-single digit growth. But for us, our business is to continue to add the right tenants that can add to the center, and we're going to continue to do that, and we'll continue to redevelop appropriately on many of these centers as well over the number of years ahead.

Operator: The next question will come from Michael Mueller with J.P. Morgan. Please go ahead.

Michael Mueller: Yeah. Hi. Thanks. Can you give us a sense as to, for Sagefield, what the cost is, and what sort of yield are you expecting to achieve with that? And just one last one to tack onto it, just what sort of pre-leasing levels did you need to see to pull the trigger on it to start it?

Eli Simon: Yeah. I would say, right now, don't feel comfortable saying the cost. The yield's the same, in line with all the other stuff we're doing. The pre-lease, it's less a specific number. It's more art than science. We don't have a number that approves deals. It's a number and the names of certain tenants. We want to have certain tenants that we think are really important. It's where the conversations are with the other tenants, what's the LOI, how deep is the pool behind those tenants. It's definitely more art than science, but it is going to be very, very exciting. We're honestly ecstatic about it. It's going to be like something that we have not built before.

You'll see some renderings, I think, when we do our groundbreaking next week. But it's an awesome market. It's the path of growth, and the tenant demand has been really strong. We're excited, and hopefully excited for everyone to come see it in a couple years' time.

Operator: The next question will come from Alexander Goldfarb with Piper Sandler. Please go ahead.

Alexander Goldfarb: Hey. Good evening out there. Eli, I think you mentioned the new program called One Simon, if I have that correctly. It sort of combines all the brands. But bigger topic is, I've asked you this about the Simon Brand Ventures and increasing the ancillary income, but as social media, AI, and everything accelerates at a pretty incredible clip, how is it that you guys think about trying to monetize more of the way that people interact with the malls versus, obviously, your retailers, they are doing the same thing. Everyone has their own social media, everyone has their own AI. Now you have AI agents that go shopping for you, who find the best deals.

How do you guys look at this so that, one, you can sort of maximize the revenue that you have, but two, that you are not sort of competing with your customers, who are your tenants?

Eli Simon: Sure. Thanks, Alex. First, the campaign, It's a Simon Thing. I encourage you guys to look at it on our social media channels. It is a very good campaign, been very well received, and it is exciting. When I think about our ancillary business, to me, I think there is a tremendous opportunity here. When we went through these budget meetings on these properties the last two weeks, what we have seen is that I think we are just scratching the surface. I do not think it is really cannibalization with our retailers. It is complementary for them. What they want is they want footsteps in their doors, and our job is to bring footsteps to the mall.

Then there is now an ability, we think, to even drive a specific footfall to these tenant stores. That is a combination of our digital presence, it is a combination of our database, it is a combination of our physical presence at the malls, and at our Premium Outlets, and at our Mills properties. It is a pretty exciting time. It is early days, obviously, with the Simon Media Network that literally we launched, or signed, I should say, some of our first contracts in the last week or so. It really is complementary to the retailers, because at the end of the day, everybody wants more traffic and everybody wants more sales, and that helps everybody.

We are on the same team. We view our retailers as partners, and we think we have a mousetrap now, not just obviously in the physical footprint in terms of a mall, but in our digital channels and our databases to help drive traffic to their stores.

Then the other thing is, obviously, we think that is an incredibly interesting mousetrap, so to speak, for non-retailers too, for companies in the travel space, for companies in the financial services space, and in the hospitality space, who look at it and say, "We have the most well-qualified customers because they are here to shop and to spend." So we are having conversations not just with our tenants, but with also brands and companies outside of our retail ecosystem. So, it is exciting, it is growing, and hopefully it is growing significantly more in the years to come.

I don't really read too much into that. The junior brands are similar in both. The athleisure brands are growing in both. Men's and women's apparel are growing in both. That's really the only one of note. I would say the border is growing again.

Both the Canadian border and the Mexican border assets and the U.S. side of it are growing, maybe a little bit lower than the portfolio overall, which again, those are more outlets than malls, and that again, kind of skews that outlet versus mall number. It's really all positive.

Again, I'd like Woodbury to grow a bit more than it has, like the border assets to grow a bit more than they are, but it's overall no other real weak spots in growth. As far as the stores, I mean, listen, tenant demand is very strong, and that demand is broad-based. It is the national retailers that have been in our centers, in our portfolio for a number of years. It's new emerging brands, it's restaurants, it's the best local and regional operators. When we look at it, the demand is strong, and I think they are realizing that you have to have stores at the best centers to grow a business.

E-commerce is great, but without stores to control your brand presence, to control your distribution, direct-to-consumer distribution in the physical world, it's hard to really be a successful modern brand today. I think that's led to this pipeline that is up 30% year-over-year, but the national retailers that have been in our portfolio for decades, that group, it's up 40%. What they're seeing is that we need to open stores, and we need to open stores throughout the portfolio in the best centers, and with a landlord that they can trust, who's going to reinvest into their space, into their center, and bring traffic. I think it's all good news from the leasing perspective.

Callaway (CALY) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Debt reduction and margin gains lifted results despite weaker golf rounds.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Callaway Golf Company (CALY -4.10%) reported third quarter results that management indicated were better than expected as the company completed its first three quarters as a pure-play golf equipment entity. Management reported that the divestiture of Jack Wolfskin and a 60% stake in Topgolf allowed for a significant balance sheet transformation, including a $1.4 billion debt reduction and the implementation of a $200 million share repurchase program. The company stated it is now focusing on structural gross margin improvements through SKU rationalization, supply chain efficiencies, and strategic shifts in product launch cycles, such as lengthening iron life cycles to improve profitability. The company increased its full-year revenue and EBITDA guidance to reflect operational leverage and strong consumer response to new technology platforms despite weather-related declines in regional rounds played.

To withdraw your question, please press star then 2. Please note this event is being recorded. This returned us to being a pure play golf equipment company. As we made this transition, we recognized the opportunity that it created to refocus on our core business and make meaningful changes and improvements. These transactions quickly allowed us to transform our balance sheet and our capital allocation strategy. Accordingly, as we entered this year, we announced a new $200 million share repurchase program which we have been steadily executing against.

With approximately $42 million in repurchases in both of the first half of this year and approximately $50 million this last quarter. We have been researching this technology for approximately 7 years now. And producing both prototypes and custom models internally since late 2022. We believe this technology is going to be transformative for the club category. And this investment positions us for leadership in this area. We expect the new facility will come online mid next year. And beginning in 2028, it will support a portion of our US product needs focusing on custom titanium product. You will also further support and develop our R&D expertise in this area.

For many years now, there have been no scaled vendors or competitors manufacturing titanium clubs in the US. And to the best of our knowledge, outside of China, there is no scaled capacity for the 3D printing of titanium clubs using the complex engineering designs and specifications that we require. We intend to lead the golf industry in the development of this technology and this will be the first facility to support US production of golf clubs in many years. Zooming in even a little closer, a portion of next year's product line will also feature this technology. And it will do so in an interesting and productive way that benefits performance.

We look forward to announcing that product to the world in early 27. Discussing it with you on our next call. And to unlocking the full potential of this new technology platform over the next several years. Now turning to our recent results. Q3 revenue was $437 million. Down 6% year over year due to our previously announced decision to launch less product in the second half of this year, but ahead of expectations due to stronger than anticipated acceptance of the products we did launch during the quarter. Adjusted EBITDA was $36 million up 17% versus the prior year and also above expectations.

For the 9 months year to date, revenue was up 3%, adjusted EBITDA increased 31%, and EPS is up 144%. We believe this revenue performance was slightly better than the broader market across both the Callaway and TravisMathew brands and the earnings growth demonstrates the operating leverage, and execution benefits of our more focused golf platform. Given the current amount of macroeconomic and geopolitical uncertainty, we are pleased with these market conditions.

This product is already on tour, and will be launched globally later this month. Turning to the apparel and gear segment.

Which has increased significantly over 2025 costs.

In addition, the continued conflict in the Middle East has led to increased petrochemical based cost pressures, including increased energy costs for us and our suppliers, increased freight costs including fuel surcharges, and increased petrochemical based raw material costs, such as those used in golf balls. These cost pressures are included in the guidance we provided today And so far, we have been able to manage them in a manner that has not had an material negative impact on our operations or financial results. We will continue to monitor developments in this area and report back as appropriate. Now turning to our full year and fourth quarter 26 outlook.

Consensus Cloud (CCSI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Corporate growth and debt paydown drove another quarter of gains.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Consensus Cloud Solutions, Inc. (CCSI -1.91%) reported a fourth consecutive quarter of year-over-year growth in revenue, adjusted EBITDA, and adjusted EPS, primarily supported by a corporate channel that now accounts for approximately two-thirds of total revenue. Management reported that the business transformation over the five years since its spin-off has resulted in a debt reduction of approximately $250 million and cumulative free cash flow generation of $476 million. The company is currently prioritizing the retirement of its 6.5% notes, which are expected to be called in Oct. 2027, while focusing capital allocation on mandatory debt repayment and share buybacks. Strategic growth is increasingly centered on healthcare and the public sector, with revenue from the company's Harmony initiative expected to begin contributing in 2028.

In 2021, the company had revenue that was a majority SOHO, an emerging presence in the healthcare ecosystem, a thin staffing of 450 people, and $805 million of debt, which was 4x our gross debt to EBITDA. In those 5 years, we have flipped the business to approximately 2/3 corporate and 1-third SOHO, while maintaining an adjusted EBITDA margin of more than 50%. Almost 2/3 of our corporate business is from the healthcare ecosystem. Almost doubled our rate of investment into the business from pre spin levels yet still generated approximately $476 million in cumulative free cash flow.

We are moving beyond basic document transport into active workflow management converting unstructured data into structured actionable clinical tasks. By ingesting documents from eFax and other sources, Harmony, built on Clarity and DocHealth, manages essential workflows outside the EHR, prior authorizations and referral follow ups. While still in its infancy stage, the initial results are compelling. 1 early adopter customer reduced patient triage time from up to 40 days down to an average of just 2. Exceeded their original target of 4 to 5 days driving massive improvement in both speed to care and provider economics.

The broader government ecosystem, including federal, state, local, education, and regulated contractors. As long as SOHO produces robust, highly efficient cash flow, it is fulfilling its exact purpose. To summarize, our team is executing well against our priorities. At least 10 local governments have moved to drop Flock’s surveillance cameras since summer

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

At least 10 local governments and police departments have moved since this summer to curb, restrict, or end their use of Flock Safety technology as concerns about mass surveillance and data privacy continue to grow. The backlash is part of a broader pushback against the rapid adoption of advanced AI technology, including the data center buildout, facial recognition, and other uses of AI.

Flock, founded in 2017, sells surveillance software — like license plate readers, drones, and microphone-based edge-computing devices to pick up gunshots and other sounds — that can help solve crimes, such as finding missing people or stolen cars. More than 100,000 Flock cameras are in use nationwide.

But in the past three months, Monroe, Ohio; Newburgh, New York; Richmond, Virginia; Hopkinton, Massachusetts; New Bedford, Massachusetts; and Lansing, Michigan, have all moved to end their contracts with Flock. Meanwhile, police in East Bridgewater and Grafton, Massachusetts, also announced plans to stop using Flock cameras. Just this week, residents of League City, Texas, voted against their police department’s continued use of Flock’s license plate readers, and the cameras are now set to be removed.

The concerns range from privacy to how data is shared (and with whom) to procurement concerns.

In Asheville, North Carolina, police lost access to Flock after the city voted to terminate its contract in August, and the El Paso County Sheriff’s Office in Colorado announced in September that it would stop using Flock cameras. When asked for comment, the El Paso County Sheriff’s Office redirected TechCrunch to its press release, where Sheriff Joseph Roybal said that discontinuing the use of Flock was the right decision for the county.

“We have the tools, training, and experience to locate and apprehend criminals without relying on Flock cameras,” he said in the press release.

Tatiana Rice, a senior director for U.S. legislation at The Future of Privacy Forum, told TechCrunch that the backlash to Flock isn’t surprising and only further represents the “growing public skepticism toward technology.” Flock is a target because people can see the sensors and cameras as they drive along roads; data centers have also become physical embodiments of the resentment growing against emerging technology. “A camera at the end of your street is tangible in a way that everyday sharing and selling of personal data online is not,” Rice said.

At the state level this summer, for example, Florida barred license plate readers from state highways, while Texas paused state funding for Flock. The issues again range from data privacy concerns to misuse of the technology.

Misuse has been a real problem. In August, for example, an officer in Brentwood, Missouri was accused of using Flock cameras to stalk his ex-wife during their divorce. In September, an officer from East Texas was sentenced to prison for using Flock to stalk his ex-girlfriend and her family.

Another growing concern is how immigration officials have been able to access the technology. One case that captures the issue came in March, when Ithaca, New York, passed a resolution to terminate its contract with the Flock Group.

In the resolution, the city said there were “proven instances of Flock Safety infrastructure being used by federal agents for information purposes even without specific authorizations from the contracting locality,” and that the city had reviewed reports that “either intentionally or through its negligence,” Flock had allowed U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF) to “use query results from its database of state and local ALPR [automatic license plate reader] data to investigate and persecute immigrants throughout the United States.”

“Every day, police departments across the country are using Flock technology to solve serious crimes, locate suspects, find missing people, and make a measurable impact on public safety,” Flock told TechCrunch in a statement. “While we respect every community’s decision, removing a tool that is actively helping solve violent crimes and protect communities moves public safety backward. Every Flock customer owns its data and controls who it is shared with and how it can be used. Flock does not sell data and does not have a contract with ICE.”

This August, Flock announced it would shorten the default period it stores data for new customers, from 30 days to seven. It also said agencies now need a case code before they can search the database.

The conversation around Flock echoes, in many ways, the fight over data centers. Many of these cities adopted Flock just a few years ago, and residents likely had no idea it was being installed, Adam Schwartz of the Electronic Frontier Foundation, a digital privacy nonprofit that has investigated Flock before, told TechCrunch.

That lack of awareness is similar to how data centers are popping up across the country, often before residents understand what is happening. Schwartz said police departments often decide to use Flock on their own, with little oversight from city councils. In South Windsor, Connecticut, according to local reports, Flock cameras went live last year without public warning or a vote. In October, the town voted to remove its Flock cameras.

“Grassroots activists across the country have been demanding that city councils exercise their procurement power to refuse to pay for ALPRs,” Schwartz said, referring to councils’ power to decide what the city buys. He added that activists have been fighting for years and that “many city councils are listening.”

The main argument for technology like Flock is that it helps combat crime and keep neighborhoods safe. And many local governments are not dropping Flock or similar technology; instead, they are adding restrictions on its use or looking for alternatives.

Cleveland, Ohio, for example, voted this summer to extend its contract with Flock, but has started restricting searches related to immigration. It has also limited access to the database to the Cleveland Police alone, cutting off the Northeast Ohio Regional Fusion Center, essentially a federal intelligence agency, which had initially been given access.

“They are used and needed for investigative purposes,” the city said when reached for comment. “The Department of Public Safety is currently reviewing responses to an RFP [request for proposals] that was sent out to other vendors that provide the same technology as Flock.”

But as with data centers, communities want more say in the trade-offs. “These tools can offer real public safety benefits, but communities are clearly interested in ensuring that those benefits are not at the cost of their civil liberties,” Rice said. Surveillance technology will likely remain a political hot topic, especially since Democrats now control Congress.

In September, President Donald Trump said he liked Flock’s technology “because of law enforcement,” even though a Reuters poll that same month found that more Americans dislike the technology than like it.

Also that month, Rep. Thomas Massie, a Republican from Kentucky, introduced a bill called the Flock-Off Act to bar the use of federal money to buy “Flock-style surveillance” and put other safeguards in place. In October, Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.), Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-N.Y.), and Sen. Jeff Merkley (D-Ore.) introduced the Ban Flock Act to end “the rise of AI-powered surveillance cameras.”

Why Did Global Markets Plunge After the US Midterm Elections?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shubham Vishwakarma is a crypto market analyst and technical content writer who covers price action, on-chain signals, and breaking blockchain news. He simplifies complex market data into sharp, easy-to-understand insights, helping readers stay ahead of trends in Bitcoin, altcoins, and DeFi. His writing combines technical precision with compelling market storytelling.

The S&P 500 fell 4.4%, Nasdaq dropped 5.5%, and Dow Jones lost more than 1,800 points on November 5.

AI infrastructure concerns and uncertainty over data-center regulations intensified selling in semiconductor and technology stocks.

South Korea's KOSPI plunged more than 8%, triggering a temporary trading halt as the sell-off spread across Asian markets.

Global markets suffered a sharp sell-off on November 5 as investors reassessed the outlook for artificial intelligence (AI) investment, semiconductor demand and data-center expansion.

The S&P 500 fell 4.4%, the Nasdaq Composite dropped 5.5%, and the Dow Jones lost more than 1,800 points. The decline also spread to Asia, where South Korea’s KOSPI plunged more than 8% on Friday. While Democratic election victories raised concerns about tighter AI infrastructure regulation, growing doubts over technology valuations and investment returns added to the pressure.

Technology and semiconductor stocks led Thursday’s losses. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index dropped more than 12%, while Nvidia fell around 9% and AMD declined approximately 17%.

Investors are increasingly questioning whether massive AI infrastructure investments will generate sufficient returns. Concerns over high valuations, financing costs and the profitability of data-center projects have added pressure to the sector.

Democratic victories in the midterm elections have raised expectations of greater scrutiny of AI infrastructure development. Proposals to restrict or pause new data-center projects have created uncertainty around expansion plans.

Potential delays could affect semiconductor manufacturers, cloud providers and companies supplying networking and power equipment. UBS analysts have warned that a portion of planned US data-center capacity additions in 2028 could face disruption from construction restrictions.

The pressure spread to Asian markets on Friday. South Korea’s KOSPI plunged more than 8%, triggering a temporary trading halt, while semiconductor companies Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix faced heavy selling.

🚨THE LARGEST SINGLE-DAY WIPEOUT EVER!🇺🇸 $2,800,000,000,000 has been wiped out from the US stock market today, marking the largest single-day loss in history.The previous record was $2,690,000,000,000 during the COVID-19 market crash. pic.twitter.com/vgFeKnSvMS

The decline highlights how closely Asian markets are tied to the global AI supply chain. Weakness in US technology stocks has quickly translated into pressure on semiconductor manufacturers across the region.

President Donald Trump blamed the Democrats’ election victories for Thursday’s market decline, highlighting the impact on retirement savings and 401(k) accounts.

However, the sell-off reflects several overlapping concerns, including AI spending, semiconductor demand, elevated valuations and uncertainty surrounding data-center regulation.

Investors will now watch technology companies’ capital expenditure plans, semiconductor demand and developments around data-center approvals. Any indication of slower AI infrastructure investment could keep pressure on technology stocks.

Top 12 most fouled players in the EFL Championship

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With an ever-increasing emphasis on utilising set pieces throughout the footballing landscape, a player's ability to draw fouls and win menacing dead-ball situations is only growing in importance and the trend has not avoided the Championship.

Players who frequently win free kicks, of course, are often among the chief danger men for their side and strike the sort of fear into opponents that makes foul play a near-unavoidable necessity, and there are some at Championship level that routinely end up on the receiving end of some almighty tackles.

There are, however, some high-volume foul-winners that may just surprise you, too, as it's not just the lightning wingers, inventive playmakers or troublesome frontmen who draw the most illegal challenges in the second-tier.

With that in mind, then, Football League World has decided to explore the TWELVE most fouled players across the Championship right now, using data-led website PlayerStats for all accompanying statistics.

Getting the ball rolling in proceedings is Watford playmaker Iker Bravo, who has established himself as a rare bright spark at Vicarage Road and a thorn in second-tier defences aplenty since joining the Hornets from Udinese over the summer.

Still aged just 21, the Spanish youth international and ex-Bayer Leverkusen prodigy has translated some of his pedigree into a struggling Watford side and, with one goal and three assists on his stat-line to date, is often a threat for Championship defenders.

His close control and confident ball-carrying skills are two of Bravo's strongest qualities, and they can be too much to live with as the Hornets technician has won 23 fouls at an average of just over two per 90 minutes.

Another Spanish attacking midfielder lands next up in the form of Fer Lopez, with the Wolves sensation having lit up the Championship this season following the Old Gold's relegation from the Premier League.

Lopez, who moved to Molineux in a reported £19 million deal from Celta Vigo in the summer of 2025, is quite evidently too good for the level with six goals and three assists from his opening 13 appearances, and the classy creative kingpin's tricky flair, deft touches, line-breaking passing and venomous ball-striking mean he's one of the most feared forward players in the league right now.

Through AI, we'll rank the top 25 most intimidating football stadiums in British football, but which stadium comes out on top?

It's no surprise, then, that opponents are often desperate to prevent the Spaniard wreaking havoc, which is rarely quite enough in truth, and he's currently averaging 2.05 fouls per 90 minutes from 23 in total - representing the most marginal advantage over his fellow countryman in Bravo.

Glen Kamara, by contrast, is not of the maverick number-10 genre akin to Bravo and Lopez, and that makes it somewhat surprising that he's winning even more fouls than the gifted Spanish pair.

He still likes to get forward, of course, and the former Leeds United promotion winner's ability to dictate the tempo, shield possession, drive forward and break lines from deeper areas offers a welcome contrast to the steely presence of Martinique international Jonathan Varane in QPR's two-man midfield engine room.

Those attributes are why midfield counterparts frequently look to nullify Kamara before he makes something happen or distributes possession to one of the Hoops' key difference-makers in the final third, with the experienced midfielder having also won 23 fouls at a higher average of 2.24 p90.

The QPR star hasn't drawn quite as many fouls as his namesake down at Portsmouth, though, with Abu Kamara surfacing as one of the most fouled Championship players so far this season by being brought down on 24 occasions to date.

Interestingly, Kamara's average of 1.94 represents the lowest of the 12 listed players on a per-90 minutes basis, and the winger has endured mixed fortunes since sealing an emotional return to Pompey in the summer.

With zero goals and just one assist from 14 matches and more than 1,000 minutes of league action, the 23-year-old needs to refine his final ball but still strikes fear into opposing full-backs with his electric pace and quick, tricky feet.

Among the most fouled Championship players in recent years, it's no surprise to see Callum O'Hare make the cut after switching from Sheffield United to Wrexham at the end of the previous window.

Cunningly creative with the ability to ghost past opponents in tight pockets of space, the 28-year-old playmaker loves travelling upfield in possession and can win fouls as if they're going out of fashion - having already drawn 24 at an average well in excess of two per 90.

O'Hare, who recorded 16 goal involvements during an influential final campaign at Bramall Lane last time out before joining Wrexham for £7 million, often attracts strong challenges from opponents in their desperate efforts to prevent the talented playmaker from making things happen, although he's now set to spend the "foreseeable future" on the sidelines after recently suffering ankle damage.

One of just two defenders to feature is a former teammate of O'Hare's in Sam McCallum, whose resurgence at Sheffield United over the last twelve months has made the dynamic left-back increasingly difficult to face off against for his opposite number.

McCallum's forward-first approach, marauding overlapping runs and searing pace up and down the left-hand side mean his inclusion is perhaps less surprising than you would first think, and despite balancing minutes with Harrison Burrows, the ex-Coventry City defender is among the most frequently-fouled Championship players by drawing 24 at a superior average to O'Hare.

An ex-teammate of McCallum's and a current colleague of O'Hare at Wrexham, the aggressive nature and towering presence of Kieffer Moore has always rendered the evergreen frontman as a complete foul-magnet.

He's rarely guilty of committing them, and loves to engage in the dark arts of the game with physical battles and the odd stray elbow for good measure.

Crucially, though, Moore knows exactly how to win fouls and refuses to moderate his brawny all-out approach at the ripe old age of 34, receiving 27 fouls in fewer than 1,000 minutes of football.

Ironically enough, Jack Moylan could end up becoming the first Cardiff City player to score 20 times in a single season since Kieffer Moore's goal-laden debut campaign for the Bluebirds back in 2020/21, and much like the Welshman, he also knows a thing or two about drawing fouls.

Moylan, however, wins them through different means, with the Republic of Ireland international's ability to hold onto possession, drive forward and dumbfound defenders with no shortage of flicks and tricks simply proving too hot to handle in the Championship thus far.

A summer signing from Lincoln City, the versatile attacker has already cemented his worth among the second-tier's top dogs with nine goals - including a recent hattrick against title favourites West Ham United - and his high-volume shooting tendencies mean that defenders invariably try and stop him before it's too late.

Despite Moylan drawing close to 30 fouls and more than two per match, though, nobody in the Championship can contain the Irish sensation right now.

Next up, it's fellow Cardiff star Perry Ng to feature with an ever-so-slightly higher average than his teammate, and the defensive stalwart's inclusion admittedly represents more of a surprise.

Ng has the technical quality and composure on the ball to frequently advance forward from right-back and join attacking moves, often acting in tandem with lightning-quick winger Ollie Tanner in what is very much a possession-centred approach under Brian Barry-Murphy despite lacking the raw pace to burn past opponents.

He's very streetwise, however, and alongside being a regular customer for bookings, the 30-year-old excels in the dark arts and has quite the education when it comes to winding up opposition players and drawing fouls as a result.

That's why Ng, while resembling a different profile to many of his listed counterparts, is the joint-fourth-most fouled player throughout the Championship, having won no fewer than 28 at a rate of 2.19 p90.

There are no such surprises when it comes to Leo Scienza, who was the most fouled player in the entire division during the previous campaign and is right up there yet again this term.

One of the most technically-gifted footballers outside of the Premier League, the Brazilian winger's ability to glide past opposition full-backs with consummate ease makes him a joy to watch and often lights up Tonda Eckert's promotion-chasing Southampton side and, more often than not, subjects his unfortunate rival number to a 90-minute nightmare.

Scienza, who perhaps would've hoped to boast a greater production than his two goals and two assists apiece at this moment in time but has played the second-fewest minutes of anybody on this list, remains just as difficult to defend against when he gets going with his trademark breezing runs down the left flank, and has been fouled on 29 occasions at an average of just under three times a game.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Define a common portfolio scorecard spanning cost-to-serve, margin, renewals, attach and upgrade movement, and campaign influence. Align definitions for AI deflection, autonomous resolution and time-to-guidance where relevant; use the scorecard to improve decisions, not simply report activity.

Set the portfolio governance model, decision standards and accountability expectations. Partner with offer program leaders and operational coordination support to embed the models in business rhythms, resolve cross-portfolio tensions and communicate defensible consolidated positions.

L'apertura della Gazzetta: "Neuro Milan. Amorim duro: 'Indifendibili'"

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

L'edizione odierna della Gazzetta dello Sport titola così stamattina in prima pagina: "Neuro Milan. Amorim duro: 'Indifendibili'". Brutto ko ieri sera del Diavolo contro il Ferencvaros che ha espugnato San Siro per 2-0. Per i rossoneri, autori di una prestazione molto deludente, si tratta della terza sconfitta in quattro partite di Europa League. Nel post-partita, Ruben Amorim è stato molto duro con la sua squadra: "Troviamo le ragioni e troviamo le soluzioni per risolvere questo problema. Ma non sono io a dirlo, ma è in base ai risultati, perché qui posso dire quello che voglio. Dico che stiamo dimostrando di non essere pronti, che abbiamo ancora molto lavoro da fare e che ci serve tempo per allenarci, per cambiare la nostra mentalità. Questa è la mia sensazione, ma non sono io a sostenerlo: sono i fatti che lo dimostrano. E la partita di oggi ne è stata la prova: non siamo pronti mentalmente. Non c'entra nulla l'aspetto tecnico. Non c'entra il turnover. Non potete venirmi a raccontare che la formazione titolare non era all'altezza per vincere la partita. Tutte le occasioni concesse, specialmente all'inizio, ce le siamo regalate da soli. Ricordo certe reazioni quando perdevamo palla: non erano affatto le stesse che abbiamo visto tre giorni fa, e avevamo avuto quattro giorni per riposare.

Quindi non sono io a parlare, è quello che stiamo mostrando in campo. E se la squadra parla, non sono io a farlo. Dico semplicemente quello che vedo, perché voi non siete stupidi e io non voglio prendere in giro i miei giocatori. Quindi ho tutte le ragioni per difendere la squadra, e quando parlo in questi termini mi assumo la responsabilità in primis io, perché la squadra... è una mia responsabilità. Ma non possiamo permetterci di fare una cosa del genere, soprattutto nel primo tempo, davanti ai nostri tifosi, a casa nostra. Non possiamo farlo, dobbiamo migliorare, altrimenti finisce qui".

Milam, Krosche spinge per Hardung. Il mercato di gennaio tra difesa e attacco: Inacio, Tresoldi e la situazione di Camarda

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In un video sul suo canale YouTube, Fabrizio Romano, insieme a Matteo Moretto, ha dato alcuni aggiornamenti sulle novità in dirigenza al Milan e sul mercato di gennaio del club rossonero.

"La trattativa per Krosche è stata un'altalena. È una trattativa che di fatto il Milan aveva praticamente già definito prima del mercato estivo. Poi per una questione di tempistiche e una questione anche economica, perché comunque l'Eintracht Francoforte chiedeva un indennizzo molto superiore rispetto a quello che in queste ore il Milan ha pagato o comunque che pagherà a breve. Comunque sì ci siamo: Marcus Krosche sarà il nuovo Head of Football del Milan. Il Milan pagherà un indennizzo tra i 3 e i 4 milioni di euro. L'ufficialità, da quanto mi risulta, arriverà ai primi di gennaio. Però è chiaro che Krosche già in questi mesi inizierà a lavorare dietro le quinte per il Milan, poi da gennaio in modo ufficiale sarà il nuovo dirigente rossonero".

"Krosche si inserisce in una struttura organizzativa e sportiva che Cardinale aveva già pensato una volta ristrutturato il Milan da zero. Ci sono altre figure all'interno della struttura Milan, come per esempio Bobby Gardiner, Almstadt, che da quanto mi risulta resteranno, però è chiaro che la figura di Krosche sarà predominante e a livello sportivo sarà quella con maggiori poteri. Da quanto mi risulta, Krosche continua a spingere per cercare di portare un direttore sportivo che possa aggiungersi all'organigramma e il nome che continua a frullare nella testa di Krosche è sicuramente quello del suo braccio destro Timmo Hardung, che è l'attuale direttore sportivo dell'Eintracht. Però è ancora da capire se Timo Hardung resterà all'Eintracht al 100%. È vero che l'Eintracht sta cercando di dargli più poteri possibili, però non escludo un'uscita di Hardung a partire da gennaio o da febbraio. Il Milan resta alla finestra, su Hardung ci sono anche altri club tedeschi come Norimberga, Hannover o Amburgo. Il Milan vuole provare ad inserire un'altra figura, magari un po' più avanti".

"Sicuramente un difensore arriverà. Doveva già arrivare in estate, arriverà a gennaio, insomma non è un mistero. Il Milan l'ha cercato anche con insistenza durante il mercato estivo, un difensore in più ovviamente oltre a Mario Gila che tra l'altro è stato un ottimo investimento del Milan, per cui l'idea è di metterne dentro almeno un altro. Il Milan ha reintegrato Tomori un minuto dopo la fine del mercato estivo per necessità, ma l'idea del Milan di prendere un difensore in più. Per cui sicuramente un difensore arriverà. Il nome di Gonçalo Inacio va sempre tenuto lì perché è un nome che è nella testa del Milan dal primo giorno del mercato estivo, da quando è arrivato Ruben Amorim fino all'ultimo minuto del mercato è sempre stato comunque nelle idee. Ci sono stati tentativi diversi con formule diverse. Il Milan addirittura aveva pensato a un certo punto a fine agosto a un possibile prestito molto oneroso con un diritto di riscatto, quindi si sono pensate più soluzioni che lo Sporting non ha accettato. Tra l'altro Sporting ha cambiato pelle, è arrivato Paolo Fonseca in panchina, dunque vedremo quanto questo potrà incidere sulle dinamiche di questa operazione. Chiaramente non è l'unico nome, ma Inacio è un giocatore sicuramente graditissimo, il Milan è in ottimi rapporti con i suoi agenti che sono gli stessi di Amorim. Ma ripeto che non finisce qui, c'è una lista e il Milan farà le sue scelte".

"Nei giorni scorsi abbiamo fatto il nome di questo ragazzo alto dell'Union Saint-Gilloise, Sylla. Se ne parla molto bene, ho saputo che anche altri grandi club sono andati a vederlo in questo inizio di stagione tra Spagna e Inghilterra, quindi è veramente un calciatore molto promettente. Sulle sue tracce c'è anche il Milan. È un tipo di calciatore diverso ovviamente da Gonçalo Inacio perché chiaramente a livello di età e di esperienza è sicuramente diverso, però occhio anche ad eventuali investimenti in vista del futuro".

"È da capire la situazione di Francesco Camarda. Sicuramente il Milan dovrà capire cosa fare, si cercherà di capire effettivamente quale può essere il percorso migliore per il giovane attaccante rossonero che ha bisogno magari anche di più minutaggio. Occhio però anche ad un'altra questione perché è vero che il Milan è attento anche alle prestazioni e anche alla struttura economica dell'operazione di Nicolò Tresoldi, che è dichiaratamente un tifoso del Milan. Tra l'altro c'è un retroscena tra il Milan e Tresoldi perché anni fa il Diavolo aveva chiuso Tresoldi, poi però le parti non sono arrivate alle firme. Vedremo se a gennaio ci sarà la possibilità da parte del club rossonero di tornare con forza su di lui".

The Bosch dual-cooking air fryer syncs your food finish times, and with 6 presets, it takes the guesswork out of temperatures and times.

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

Price: £169 (U.S. pricing TBA)Capacity: 2 x 4.5-quart basketsControls: Touch buttonDimensions: 10.8"H x 17.2"W x 14.9"DModes: Air Fry, Recrisp, Bake, Roast and Keep WarmExtra features: Shake reminder, 6 custom presetsNoise level: 63 dB (max)Min to Max heat: 104-446°F (40-230°C)Weight: 24.2 pounds (11kg)

If you’re seriously into air fry cooking you might be ready to upgrade your single-drawer appliance to a double version. There's nothing more frustrating than waiting for your main to cook to finish, before starting on your side — not to mention keeping the cooked food warm.

So, if you started your air fryer life with a single-drawer appliance, you can probably see the benefit of upgrading to one of the best dual-drawer air fryers. The Bosch Series 4 Dual Drawer wants to join that list, and after testing this air fryer I think it definitely deserves a spot.

Most of the air fryers in our top picks are single-drawer models, such as the Ninja Air Fryer Pro 4-in-1, which gets 5 stars. But the Philip 3000 Dual Zone Air Fryer is currently our favorite dual-zone air fryer and gets a 4-star rating. Here's how the Bosch stacks up.

Currently, the Bosch Series 4 dual-drawer air fryer is only available in the U.K., and you'll pay £169 direct from Bosch for the model with two 4.5-liter drawers. Currently, there are no plans to launch it in the U.S. market, but if that changes, I'll make sure to update this review.

In terms of comparisons, we've also tested the Philips 3000 Dual Zone Air Fryer (mentioned above) with a 6-liter and 3-liter drawer, which is £108 at Amazon in the U.K. and $210 at Amazon in the U.S.It's also worth checking out Cosori’s 10-in-1 Dual Basket Air Fryer, which is $169 at Amazon in the U.S, while the 8-in-1 version is £119 in the U.K.

As air fryers go, this Bosch version looks smart, but it does takes up countertop space because the two equal-sized baskets sit side by side. If space is at a premium in your kitchen but you still want air-frying capacity, I’d suggest investing in a double-stack air fryer instead.

You won't be able to peer inside through a window to see how your cooking is progressing, so bear that in mind, but I think of a window as a 'nice to have' feature rather than a definite 'must have'.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The digital control panel is intuitive to use, even with dual baskets to manage. Right at the top, you’ll notice preset food icons. I don’t usually rely on presets with air fryers, but they are convenient if you regularly cook the same staples. You don’t need to think about the temperature setting, so you can adjust the cook time up or down if needed.

What presets do you get? Choose from chips, poultry, searing, fish, vegetables, and small baked items. It also has defrost, warming up and keep warm modes.

You can also add your own favorites using MyFry — I love this feature as it means you don't have to keep referring to a recipe to check the temperature or cook time of your regular favorites.

Sync or separateYou can set a different time and temperature for each basket, match the two, or sync them to finish at the same time. I found it intuitive and was able to use the controls without spending much time reading through the instructions.

TemperatureI tend to use my current air fryer, the Corsori TuroBlaze, to proof dough, as its lowest temperature is perfect at 30°C. However, you won't be able to do that with the Bosch as the temperature starts slightly higher at 40°C, with a range up to 230°C, in 5°C increments.

It made sense to start by using both cooking baskets so I could experiment with the sync setting. I placed quarter-pound burgers in one drawer and frozen fries in the other. I selected the ‘fries’ program (180°C for 15 minutes) and the shake reminder, and set the burgers at 200°C for 12 minutes, without choosing a program.

With a longer cook time, the fries started first, and the ‘Hold’ displayed above the left basket with the burgers. Then, after three minutes, the burgers started to cook. At 6 minutes (halfway through the fries cook time), the appliance beeped, and a shake reminder flashed up on the display.

I was pretty happy with both the burgers and fries, especially considering I'd piled a whole load of fries into the basket.

I do love roasting vegetables, especially peppers and zucchini, so I sliced some up and placed them both in one basket. I choose the vegetable preset, which automatically selects a temperature of 180°C for 15 minutes.

Halfway through the cook, I got a shake reminder, and I was able to check on the vegetables before giving them a shuffle and returning them to the drawer.I didn't need to adjust the cook time, and the vegetables were perfectly cooked with a pleasant char.

It’s a little different to use than most of the air fryers I’ve tested, where you select a mode such as ‘air fry’, ‘bake’ or ‘roast’. With the Bosch you get a choice of presets which are highlighted by icons on the control panel.

But I like that you don't need to think about what temperature or how long you need to cook each food item for. You can also add your own timings and temperatures for your favorites.

The cooking functionality didn’t disappoint, and I found the air fryer easy to use, but the double baskets do limit you if you like to cook foods such as a whole chicken. However, it's ideal if you plan to cook several items at once, since you won't be left with cold fries while your protein cooks.

Google's 'Design Fails' Easter egg just made my day

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's a tribute to the world's most maligned font.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We've spotted lots of Google Easter eggs over the years. Hidden animations, fun widgets and even playable games have surprised us in the search results for particular queries. Sometimes they're quickly discovered when they're designed around trending search topics, but some less obvious gems can pass under the radar, continuing to surprise unsuspecting users.

We just discovered a gem on a topic that's close to our own hearts. Here at Creative Bloq, we consider ourselves something of an authority on design fails. We've written about enough of them over the years, from disastrous rebrands to AI adverts.

So imagine our delight when we discovered that typing the term 'design fails' into Google delivers results presented in one of the world's most controversial typefaces.

I say one of the most controversial fonts, because these days we would much rather have Comic Sans back in place of that generic AI font that's cropping up on flyers everywhere.

Putting Google's joke aside, the typeface has its place, and not every use of Comic Sans is a design fail. Indeed, many designers have come out in defence of Comic Sans over the years.

It was created in 1994 by Microsoft in-house designer Vincent Connare to solve a specific design problem. He needed a suitable font for the dog in the Microsoft Bob interface – Times New Roman just didn't suit the pup's playfulness, so Vincent created a comic book-inspired font to match the cartoony design.

Mimicking informal, handwritten speech in cartoons, it was perfect for injecting levity into casual communication pieces for schools, community spaces, standing out from stuffy serifs and dull sans serifs while also being very clear and readable thanks to its wide spacing.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The problem came when people started using it in inappropriate applications, including official notices, and its infamy only grew with the 'graphic design is my passion' meme.

There he manages a team of designers, photographers and video editors who specialise in producing visual content and design assets for the hospitality sector. He also dances Argentine tango.
