Why Unai Emery is right to turn down Manchester United and stick with Aston Villa

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s a great quirk of Premier League scheduling that Manchester United welcome Aston Villa to Old Trafford this weekend, after a week in which Jamie Carragher’s suggestion that Villa manager Unai Emery would jump at the chance to move to United prompted a legal threat from the midlands club, an apology from the outspoken pundit and an announcement late on Wednesday that Emery and his bosses had ‘positive discussions establishing a clear commitment beyond 2029 for the joint development of the club’.

A new contract is reportedly imminent for the Spaniard, and who could possibly blame Emery for sticking around at a club that have repeatedly backed him, given him more control than he’ll have anywhere else and whose fans worship him for delivering their first trophy in 30 years last season.

The alternative? Joining a constant catastrophe that everyone agrees needs far more than just a new manager to put back together.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

While Emery is going nowhere, the whole affair does raise questions about football’s food chain, something which definitely does exist but is clearly going through some changes right now.

Managers will always believe they are the right person to take a once great club and bring back the glory days, and being the one to do so is an intoxicating idea.

But Emery has been here before and was burned by his experience at Arsenal where a collection of expensive failed transfers and unmotivated senior players gave him the worst possible platform to build from. Sound familiar?

All roads still lead to Real Madrid for players but the money involved in Premier League football means simply being a relatively successful English club puts you closer to the top of the chain than ever before, and given United’s penchant for finding new lows every season, a move from Villa north to Manchester is at best a sideways move these days – just ask Youri Tielemans.

Also, given the relative state of flux that many of the ‘big six’ find themselves in these days, there’s an opportunity for Emery and Villa to elbow their way in and become a more established fixture towards the top of the league.

Select your team for football news tailored to you Close

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

United are joined by Spurs in their ability to self-implode, Chelsea are chaotic at the best of times, Liverpool are still just about managing the transition to a Jurgen Klopp-less existence and Manchester City are unlikely to be challenging for titles in the near future, depending on impending sanctions.

Only Arsenal are properly set up for consistent success, and Villa clearly believe trusting in Emery is the best way to eventually get themselves into the title conversation.

There are pitfalls to their approach – if Emery does someday get his head turned by a massive paycheck in Saudi Arabia or Real Madrid do come calling, he may still move on and leave the club as a ship without a sail, which is why clubs like Brighton and Brentford treat their managers as cogs in a well-oiled system.

United fans had some temporary relief midweek with their emotional comeback against Roma in the Champions League and may well go into the international break feeling good if they manage to squeeze out a win on Sunday, but regardless of the result, Emery is in the right place.

Broadridge's Distributed Ledger Repo Processes $7.6 Trillion in October

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

October 2026 ADV reaches $345 billion as Broadridge continues to advance institutional tokenization infrastructure

NEW YORK, Nov. 6, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Broadridge (NYSE: BR), global Fintech leader, today announced that its Distributed Ledger Repo (DLR) processed an average of $345 billion in daily repo transactions during October, with volumes totaling $7.6 trillion. The total settled volume reflects the continued scale of tokenized securities financing activity as Broadridge expands its digital asset infrastructure through its recently launched DLX platform.

"DLR has laid the groundwork for the next phase of tokenization, as institutions increasingly shift their focus from exploring initial digital assets use cases to scaling these capabilities across their businesses," said Horacio Barakat, Global Head of Digital Innovation at Broadridge. "In 2027, we anticipate further integration of tokenized assets into everyday financial market operations, helping institutions unlock efficiencies across the asset lifecycle. Through DLR and our broader DLX platform, we're focused on making these capabilities more accessible and practical for financial institutions."

Building on DLR's proven infrastructure and institutional experience, DLX extends Broadridge's tokenization capabilities beyond securities financing into a broader range of assets and workflows, spanning issuance, trading, settlement, servicing and distribution. Together, DLR and DLX equip financial institutions with the trusted, resilient technology and operational controls needed to integrate tokenized assets into existing market operations, helping bridge traditional and digital markets while reducing complexity.

To learn more about DLR, the world's largest institutional platform for settling tokenized real assets, visit Broadridge's DLR.

About Broadridge's Tokenization Solutions

Broadridge is building the governance layer for tokenized equities, ensuring investors have access to trusted, institutional-grade governance as securities move on-chain. Through proxy voting, investor communications, disclosures, and corporate actions, Broadridge helps investors exercise their ownership rights across tokenized and traditional holdings, including across issuer-sponsored and third-party tokenization models.

Broadridge connects traditional and digital finance with the trusted infrastructure institutions need to put tokenization to work. DLX, its multi-chain digital asset platform, brings together capabilities across issuance, distribution, trading, financing, settlement, custody, and asset servicing. DLX builds on the production experience of Broadridge's Distributed Ledger Repo (DLR) solution, the world's largest institutional platform for settling tokenized real assets, tokenizing more than $345 billion of repo's a day.

Through its integrated platform and connected partner ecosystem, Broadridge helps issuers, banks, broker-dealers, asset managers, and wealth firms bring tokenized investments to market, mobilize collateral, and connect investors to new opportunities through existing systems and workflows. By orchestrating the ecosystem rather than requiring clients to rebuild it, Broadridge helps firms manage complexity and scale across traditional and on-chain markets.

Broadridge is a global technology leader with trusted expertise and transformative technology, helping clients and the financial services industry operate, innovate, and grow. Broadridge powers investing, governance, and communications for clients â driving operational resiliency, elevating business performance, and transforming investor experiences.

Broadridge's technology and operations platforms process and generate over 8 billion communications annually and underpin the daily average trading of over $18 trillion in tokenized and traditional securities globally. Chainalysis says breach exposed password hashes and live tokens

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chainalysis disclosed password hashes and live tokens were exposed after initially reporting only non-sensitive customer data was accessed.

Chainalysis on Wednesday disclosed a wider impact from a cyberattack it initially described as involving only non-sensitive customer information.

“On Monday, we discovered certain customer information including names, usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all Chainalysis users were exposed to a malicious actor,” the blockchain analytics firm said in an update to its incident notice.

The company did not disclose whether each type of information was exposed for every user and declined to provide Cointelegraph with additional information beyond its published disclosure.

Chainalysis initially said on Oct. 30 that the intrusion exposed user IDs, names and email addresses but found no evidence that attackers accessed authentication credentials or session tokens.

Another Wednesday update stated an “on-path attack” exposed some customers’ passwords. The company said it had contained and remediated that attack vector and was locking affected accounts and requiring password resets.

Despite the broader exposure, Chainalysis said it still had no evidence that attackers accessed investigation data or manipulated its attribution data.

Why managers can learn a lot from leaders like Napoleon - Pulis

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The November international break is almost upon us, which always means one thing - the managerial merry-go-round is about to properly get into gear.

It's known as 'sacking season' for a reason - the new campaign has started to take shape and I know from experience that any manager who is not winning games will always be sleeping with just one eye closed, and the other one open.

At any club on a poor run of results, the owners and supporters will be questioning whether the manager is the right fit to continue.

This two-week break from domestic games is the ideal opportunity to blood the new incumbent and give them a period of getting to know everyone before the games start up again.

That means it is 'hiring season' too, but there are always far more out-of-work managers than vacant positions.

Since the start of last season, 60 different managers have resigned or been sacked by teams in the Premier League and Football League. Three of them left to take up offers from other clubs but, in total, 37 of them - or 62% - are not currently in management.

For most managers, it's rarely the case where you walk into a new club within a few weeks of leaving your last one, so what do out-of-work managers do between jobs to make sure they are ready to take the next opportunity that fits?

Known as 'The Bald Eagle', Jim Smith was renowned as a master of winning promotion during a management career that spanned almost 40 years. He is pictured here celebrating after leading Derby into the Premier League in 1996

It is more than 32 years since I received my marching orders from my first job at Bournemouth, who were in what is now known as League One. I was devastated.

I had - as I always did - given everything I could do to benefit the club, and with it being my first sacking I honestly did not know where my next step would take me.

But, on the second day of me being out of work, my home phone rang and at the end of the line was Jim Smith, who was in charge of Portsmouth, a few miles down the south coast.

I knew Jim then, but not really well. I'd been a manager for two years, but by then he had already spent 25 years in the dugout, at eight different clubs.

He asked how I was and, before I could even reply, recited these immortal words, which always stuck with me - "son, you're not a real manager until you get sacked at least three times - so get your backside off the sofa and get on with getting another job" - then he put the phone down!

I've used that sentence myself many times since to managers who have lost their job and are struggling to come to terms with it.

That can happen a lot. People outside the game maybe don't realise how difficult it can be when you are sacked, and how it affects certain individuals differently - you can easily fall off the merry-go-round and, as a manager, you never know for sure whether you will get another chance.

Bruce Rioch only spent one season at Arsenal but was responsible for bringing a certain Dennis Bergkamp to Highbury from Inter Milan, making the legendary Dutch striker his first signing in June 1995

Someone else who phoned me up in the summer of 1994 was another great name in the game as a player and manager, Bruce Rioch.

At the time, Bruce was having a lot of success as manager of Bolton - he would get the Arsenal job a few months later - and he asked me if I would scout for him while I was trying to find my next manager's post.

He was very keen to get me involved and very helpful in keeping me busy watching games.

I was equally keen on helping Bruce and ended up scouting for him for that entire season. To this day, I owe him so much in respect of what he asked me to do, because of what I learned during that time.

Football is the best team sport in the world and the fundamental thing I picked up that season is that its 'product', the players, were the secret to success.

By that, I mean that I realised that at all levels there are good players, and at all levels there are managers working on budgets. I learned that knowing the level you were at, and placing the right players at that level who you could afford, would be an enormous factor in your chances of success, or failure.

Yes, there is more to it than that - you then also had to pick good characters, coach them well and motivate them with a driving force they could handle and enjoy - but you have to start by getting the right players for the level you are at.

The following summer, I was appointed manager of Gillingham, who had just finished 89th out of the 92 clubs across the top four divisions.

I was lucky enough to inherit a club who had quite a few players out of contract, so it enabled me to reset the first team with several players I had watched during my time scouting for Bruce and Bolton.

All the hours and miles I travelled that year paid off, with Gillingham finishing second in what is now League Two and gaining promotion in my first season. Those players who we recruited kick-started four years of my managerial career in Kent, and left me with some fantastic on-field memories.

Napoleon (left) rose through the ranks to become the Emperor of the French as well as building a reputation as one of the great military commanders, and inspired Pulis (right) with his leadership skills

My time and success at Priestfield opened up an ever-revolving door of work and rest.

Whenever I was out of work in the years that followed, watching games still occupied my midweeks and weekends for the same reasons as before - I wanted to watch players who I might end up signing.

Lots of games I went to were watched from the terraces with either home or away supporters, which was always interesting and illuminating - I've always valued the views of match-going fans on different players.

Down the years, I was also invited to spend a few weeks coaching abroad, in Kuwait and the United States, by friends I had in the game.

Whatever I was doing, I always felt like I was learning, and not just by watching football around the world. I became an avid reader of sports books and also finding out about great leaders of men through history.

So, as well as visiting Bayer Leverkusen, Athletic Bilbao and RB Leipzig - who back then had only just started their rise from the bottom tier of German football - I would analyse and investigate historic battles or events.

Napoleon Bonaparte really kept me busy. His attention to detail and his command of his generals was only superseded by his troop's loyalty to him - they would literally die for him!

His 250th birthday was celebrated in Corsica in the summer of 2019, and a French newspaper invited me to go along and give a British perspective on how we see Napoleon as a leader and a person.

I was delighted to accept and was amazed by how many people from all over Europe turned up too.

Hated by many countries, but still adored by his followers, Napoleon also produced the Napoleonic Code, which is still the foundation of French civil law today.

The Charge of the Light Brigade was a disastrous assault by British cavalry led by Lord Cardigan against Russian forces during the Battle of Balaclava in 1854

As well as great military triumphs, I studied defeats too - they are as much a part of football management as victories, after all - and you can compare the organisational brilliance of Napoleon to the disaster of the Battle of Balaclava and the Charge of the Light Brigade.

One summer, I travelled out to Crimea to witness the scene of that tragic charge by the bravest of brave men.

Lord Raglan, the British commander-in-chief, was based on the high ground and could see everything in the Russian positions and the brigades.

His order to attack was ambiguous and although Lord Cardigan, who led the charge, questioned it, he still followed it through - leading to one of the most infamous military disasters in history.

It should never have happened but, just as in football, leadership and communication from the top was key. The instructions were passed down from Raglan to Lord Lucan to Cardigan and became garbled - too many cooks, in my opinion!

The last book which really stuck with me, in terms of inspiring me as a leader, was the story of HMS Endurance - disaster strikes during a journey through the ice fields of the southern hemisphere when the ship becomes stuck.

I cannot do justice to the deeds of Sir Ernest Shackleton in this column but his leadership of his men, along with his number two Frank Worsley, was extraordinary.

Both dovetailed in a way that gave their men the energy, desire and determination to believe and follow instructions in the most appalling conditions, never once questioning their leaders' abilities to help them survive.

Over the years, I've never stopped reading about, or watching, great leaders like that - there is so much that young coaches can learn from other sports or history that will benefit then, the same way it did me.

Going to games was still the most important thing when I was between jobs, because I would always believe my own eyes when watching players.

There were times when that became more of a challenge, especially at the back end of my career, but times change and if you are not in tune with what is happening in the game then you can get dropped very quickly.

But I am sure that most of my managerial compatriots would also agree with me that experience in all walks of life can be invaluable.

I learned so much when I was out of work, about all aspects of leadership, individually and collectively.

My advice to managers out of work now is the same that I received from Jim, all those years ago - pick yourselves up and get off the sofa to get back in work as quickly as possible.

You should never be afraid of dropping down a level the way I did, too. It is better to get a job, any job, than be out of football full-stop. That hasn't changed since my day, either.

Tony Pulis was speaking to BBC Sport's Chris Bevan.

‘African Kung-Fu Nazis’ Franchise Lands at Hong Kong’s Cappu Films for International Sales (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

International sales on cult martial arts splatter comedy “African Kung-Fu Nazis,” its sequel and a behind-the-scenes documentary have gone to Hong Kong-based Cappu Films.

The deal, struck with filmmaker and producer Sebastian Stein of Humanmade Films, was unveiled on the sidelines of Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) in Taipei. It covers “African Kung-Fu Nazis I,” “African Kung-Fu Nazis II” and the feature documentary “African Kung-Fu Nazis – The Doc.” It applies only to rights and territories that are still open, and leaves deals already in place untouched. Related Stories Amnesiac Surgeon Confronts His Organ-Trafficking Past in Taiwan Creative Content Fest Series ‘Hannibal Inside’ 'Lord of the Rings,' 'Avatar' VFX House Weta FX Partners With Taiwan’s Moonshine Studio Under AI Pact With TAICCA

Japanese distributor Transformer gave both features a theatrical run in Japan, opening the original in June 2021 and the sequel four years later in July 2025. Busch Media Group handled the first film’s German release on DVD, Blu-ray and VOD. The sequel played Sitges’ Brigadoon sidebar in 2025. In 2026 it went on to the Brussels International Fantastic Film Festival (BIFFF), Grossmann and Macabro, where it picked up an honorable mention.

Mixing kung fu action, gore, horror and dark comedy, the series takes its cues from vintage Hong Kong martial arts films and from Ghana’s independent filmmaking scene. In the first film, Ghanaian kung fu fighters square off against send-ups of wartime figures from Germany and Japan, among them Adolf Hitler, Hideki Tojo and “Horseman” Goering.

The sequel teams one-armed fighter Addo with Sek Tin to take on cyborg “Adolf Robotler,” who wants to become president of Ghana. Stein said the film, a Ghana-Japan-Germany production shot mostly in Ghana, was paid for largely through crowdfunding, with Japanese fans of the first film providing much of the backing.

“African Kung-Fu Nazis – The Doc” goes behind the camera on the original, following the independent shoot that started the franchise.

“With their love for Chinese kung fu, our Ghanaian heroes smash the threat of fascism,” said Stein, CEO of Humanmade Films. “Total freedom, beautiful madness. Because you won’t drink champagne if you don’t take a risk!”

Cappu and the filmmakers also plan a line of art-led merchandise, to be produced via sister company Winners Gallery and built on the franchise’s characters and look, as part of the international marketing push. Concepts and release plans have yet to be finalized.

“We love fiercely independent horror and cult films, and we want to support filmmakers who are doing something that is entirely their own,” said Janice Woo of Cappu Films. “There’s a real love of kung fu cinema in these films. We come from production ourselves, so we understand how much it takes to get a film like this made. We’re excited to work with Sebastian and help bring all three films to more audiences.”

Cappu also operates out of Beijing and handles select releases in its home market and mainland China. Its slate ranges from Tony Leung Chiu-wai crime thriller “Fox Hunt” to Shan Jiang’s “Ephemera,” a 2026 Tribeca Festival U.S. Narrative Competition premiere. The lineup also includes Carter Ward’s romance “Step Back, Doors Closing” and Francesco Lucente’s fantasy adventure “Sinbad,” which DNEG is co-financing and producing.

Taiwan Creative Content Fest Opens With Focus On IP, Co-Production & Pledges Of Government Support

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The seventh edition of Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) kicked off today with talk of bolstering Taiwan’s intellectual property (IP); reaching out to international markets through co-production and exports; and a reassuring message on government support.

Sue Wang, chair of TCCF organizer Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), opened proceedings by first acknowledging the disruption that has rocked the content industries globally over the past year, before talking about how the government agency she heads aims to provide solutions.

“The past few years have been a difficult journey for everyone, littered with many challenges,” Wang said. “At earlier editions of TCCF, we were talking about streaming and the change in consumption habits. Now we face even more changes and revolutions, which have become a given in our industry. When we create, we have to think about IP, licensing and maximizing the IP universe. It’s a challenging journey for everyone, but we will try our best to adapt.” Related Stories News Discover Asia's IPs & Co-Production Opportunities at Taiwan Creative Content Fest 2026 News Taiwan Creative Content Fest Launches Spotlight Screenings At Vieshow Cinemas

She then switched to commenting on AI, a big focus in Taiwan, which a major supplier of AI hardware through its world-class semiconductor industry. Also at TCCF, TAICCA is also signing an MOU with Weta FX to position Taiwan as an AI hub for Asia’s content industries. However, in keeping with recent narratives on the topic, she stressed the importance of human involvement in content creation. Watch on Deadline

“AI has changed all the technology and pipelines we use and is also changing how we define human society,” said Wang. “When we talk about content and culture, we have to ask whether they considered to be valuable in the future, and in turn this forces us to think about what is the quintessential quality of cultural content and what are the elements that cannot be replaced by AI.”

Taiwan’s Minister of Culture Li Yuan then stepped on to the stage to explain how Taiwan’s creative industries are benefitting from a second tranche of $303M (NTD10BN) investment from the National Development Fund (NDF).

Raising a laugh from the audience, Li described how he’d approached the head of the NDF with an “elevator pitch” for the second tranche of funding, which is currently being invested into films, series, comics, publications and animation, as well as streaming platforms and corporate entities involved in content creation.

“Many projects that we could only hope and dream about in the past are now bearing fruitful results and creating a space for Taiwanese content on the international stage,” said Li, adding that Taiwan’s growing involvement in international co-production is another key focus of government support.

Also attending the opening were executives from Weta FM, including CEO Daniel Seah and Chief Technology Officer Kimball Thurston; along with Fumio Yoshimura, CEO of Japanese studio Toei, who is delivering a keynote later today; several top executives from Taiwanese telcos and VFX houses; and representatives of trade offices in Taipei, including France, Spain, Italy, Switzerland, New Zealand and Chile.

TCCF is running November 6-9 at Taipei Nangang Exhibition Center. In addition to its usual sections – Pitching, Market and Forum – the event will feature startup fundraising pitches, the first ever Spotlight Screenings and a partnership with Golden Horse Awards to present Taiwan Emerging Actors Showcase

Along with the strategic partnership with Weta FX, TAICCA is announcing a new fund dedicated to independent digital game development with Lunta Ventures and a tie-up with IPis Innovation (Do You Still Love Me).

Meanwhile, the Taiwan Content Wave Annual Showcase will feature presentations from four investment funds – Taiwan’s Chunghwa Digital Cultural and Creative Fund, Enjoy Entertainment, Joint Journey Creative, and Momomini Kids Content Fund – which are all co-investing in content with government policy resources.

TCCF’s market section is hosting 107 exhibitors from seven countries and regions: Taiwan, South Korea, Japan, Thailand, Singapore, France, and the Philippines. Thailand is making its first appearance with a national pavilion jointly organized by the Creative Economy Agency (CEA), the Thai Animation and Computer Graphics Association (TACGA), and the Thai Association of Boys Love Content (TBLC).

Macau jails pro-democracy campaigner in city's first national security case

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Au was sentenced to 12 years and six months' jail for "subversion against the political power of the state"

Macau has sentenced an ex-lawmaker and pro-democracy campaigner to 12 years and six months' jail in the city's first national security case.

Following a closed-door trial, Au Kam San, 69, was convicted on Friday of "subversion against the political power of the state", among other offences.

His wife Virginia Cheang condemned the ruling, calling the case a "groundless persecution designed to humiliate Au".

Macau was handed back to China in 1999, ending over 400 years of Portuguese administration. Its national security law came into effect in 2009, criminalising acts of treason, secession, and subversion against the Chinese Communist Party.

The law was revised in 2023, broadening its scope to include non-violent acts of secession, and again this year, to allow for trials to be held behind closed doors.

Prosecutors had accused Au of assisting and collaborating with "external hostile organisations" by collecting, preparing and disseminating information that was "manifestly filled with political claims of a subversive nature against the state".

These took place "over a long period of time", the prosecutors claim.

Au was arrested in July 2025 and later charged with three counts of subverting the state's power, three counts of endangering national security by establishing contact with foreign organisations, and one count of breach of confidentiality.

He was found guilty of four of the seven charges following a three-day trial which ended in September. A judge had ordered that the trial be held behind closed doors "to ensure that no prejudice is caused to the interests of national security".

Macau's authorities never publicly disclosed the details of the case. Au's family had earlier told the media Au was not allowed to seek his own legal representation and had instead been assigned a lawyer by the court.

Au's wife Cheang said she hoped authorities would grant her and her family permission to regularly write to and visit Au in prison.

"I dare not hope for more as I believe that will only lead to greater disappointment," she said in an open letter on Friday.

Au, who was formerly a primary school teacher, was elected to the Legislative Assembly in September 2001. For 20 years, he served as one of only a handful of pro-democracy lawmakers in the legislature, largely made up of pro-Beijing loyalists.

Au had co-organised annual vigils in Macau to commemorate the 1989 Tiananmen Square crackdown in Beijing.

These vigils were previously tolerated in places like Macau and Hong Kong, but have effectively been banned in both cities since China imposed a sweeping national security law on Hong Kong in 2020.

Au's jailing comes a day after Hong Kong sentenced Joshua Wong, the face of its now-quashed democracy movement, to 10 years in jail. Wong, 30, whose activism started when he was just a teen, has been in custody for the last six years.

Hollywood Has Flipped on GenAI, Says ‘Avatar’ VFX House Weta FX Chief: ‘If You Don’t Have an AI Strategy, Don’t Bother’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Major Hollywood studios have gone from banning generative AI outright to shunning visual effects vendors that lack an AI plan, according to Daniel Seah, CEO of “The Lord of the Rings” and “Avatar” VFX house Weta FX.

Seah spoke to press in Taipei on Friday, after Weta FX signed a partnership with the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA) at Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF). Weta’s clients, he said, are mainly the Big Four studios and the streaming platforms. Related Stories 'Lord of the Rings,' 'Avatar' VFX House Weta FX Partners With Taiwan’s Moonshine Studio Under AI Pact With TAICCA Foot Fetish Encounter Leaves a Body in the Freezer in Singapore Dark Comedy Taiwan Creative Content Fest Project 'Build to Order'

“We also got the memo from each of the studios, especially from their legal departments, saying that there is no such scenario that you can use any of GenAI for any of our work,” Seah said of early last year. By the middle of the year, several clients were allowing it, provided Weta got pre-approval and worked with only a small number of companies. Around the turn of the year, clients began asking about Weta’s AI strategy, mostly to find out how much money the technology could save.

“It’s, ‘If you don’t have an AI strategy, don’t bother to work with us,’” Seah said of the period since the third quarter of this year.

That shift drove Weta to look for a partner that could help it move faster on GenAI. It found one in Taiwanese animation and VFX outfit Moonshine Studio, which it has worked with for around six months. Seah said AI had become a national strategy in Taiwan on the back of the island’s semiconductor industry. Moonshine, without the client restrictions Weta faced, had been moving quickly as a result, he said.

Seah said Weta has no intention of using AI to replace any of its artists or employees. “It’s more like to enhance and increase more workflow for us,” he said.

Weta chief technology officer Kimball Thurston said the company has been building its own AI models for modifying and recreating the world, “not necessarily the like ChatGPT-type of model.” Work with Moonshine is likely to focus on the artist layer – what the AI world calls the harness, “which I think is the worst name they could have chosen,” Thurston said. The goal is a tool that lets people refine their stories across “whole timelines of long-form content rather than just 20-second TikTok videos.”

Thurston also said Weta uses outside video models such as Kling AI and ByteDance’s Seedance where clients permit it. “You kind of have to,” he said. “It’s not every studio allows us to, but they also came onto the scene and killed a bunch of competition, fundamentally.” Security rules mean such models must be hosted outside China, he added. That is achievable, he said, because ByteDance now operates globally.

Under the partnership, TAICCA and Taiwan’s Ministry of Culture will identify young talent and introduce them to Weta FX. Weta may offer them internships at its offices in Wellington, Melbourne and Vancouver. Seah said Weta recently announced it is likely to open a representative office in London too.

“There’s a Taiwan contingent at Weta in Wellington already,” Thurston said. He added that those artists were there before the new deal and are “integral members of the crews.”

Weta has no footprint in Taiwan. Seah called the island the ideal base for exploring Asia. He pointed to flight times of three to four hours to Southeast Asia, Japan and Korea, government backing and a pool of engineers and storytellers. Thurston said Weta has also spoken to people in Thailand and Korea. He also pointed to a build-out of immersive experiences across Asia, possibly via Singapore.

“It’s more, to me, about market enablement than it is about any particular location,” Thurston said.

“I think this is a key opportunity to move from technology hardware to technology applications – that is, software,” TAICCA chair Sue Wang added. She called the deal the biggest Taiwan has struck in the content industry so far.

Thurston said Weta started an open-source project around 18 months ago called the Rongotai Model Train Club, named after an area of Wellington. The project tracks refinement and LoRA training and writes that usage into image metadata. Anyone defining a model has to state which datasets were used. Thurston said the effort dovetails with work at the Visual Effects Society’s Technology Committee and at MovieLabs.

In the past year, Thurston said, Weta won a data center design award for the Southern Hemisphere. The award was for a cooling system that uses no water and is more power-efficient.

“I hate that a lot of companies are building up gas, basically gas turbines to power data centers,” Thurston said. “I think it represents bad engineering, speaking as a computer scientist.”

Weta VFX supervisor Sheldon Stopsack said the new tools have to fit into the studio’s existing pipeline, including its motion-capture work. “We want to be able to kind of shape the world the way the filmmaker envisioned it, we envisioned it, and, you know, have this creative control ultimately at the end of the day with any of these technologies,” Stopsack said.

“People still want to see the big star performing,” Thurston added. “They may be performing as an ape, or they may be performing as a Na’vi, or some other crazy creature that gets created, and that creature may be created using some technique, but people want to go see the actor perform.”

Bitcoin’s rate cut relief stalls as service inflation flares

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

US services growth slowed as persistent price pressures cloud Fed rate cuts and Bitcoin's prospects for gains.

US services growth slowed in October, while the survey’s 12-month average prices index reached its highest level since April 2023. Persistent cost pressure complicates the case for rate relief that could support Bitcoin.

The Institute for Supply Management’s (ISM) October services report, released Nov. 4, put its headline PMI at 54.5, down from September’s 54.9. The reading remained above 50, indicating that services activity was still expanding.

The monthly prices index also eased to 73 from 74, but its 12-month average rose to 69.2, the highest since April 2023. The longer view points to persistent cost pressure even as the latest monthly reading retreats.

ISM’s diffusion indexes measure the direction and breadth of reported changes. A prices reading of 73 summarizes those survey responses, while an inflation rate measures the size of price changes in percentage terms. The rolling average summarizes that pressure across a longer window.

Business activity and new orders eased to 53.8 and 55.9, respectively, while the employment index rose to 51.4. Hiring strengthened alongside softer demand measures.

October’s growth and monthly prices readings eased. The 12-month prices average still reached its highest level since April 2023.

The prices index eased from 74 in September to 73 in October; it still indicated rising prices.

The average rose to 69.2, its highest since April 2023. This milestone belongs to the longer window.

A softer monthly reading can coexist with sustained cost pressure, leaving rate relief uncertain.

Diffusion indexes describe reported breadth and direction, not inflation rates. The average milestone is highest since April 2023; the monthly index declined. No survey-driven Bitcoin move or policy decision is depicted.

PMI positions use a linear, zoomed 48-58 index scale. Monthly prices use a separate linear 60-80 scale. Labels show reported September and October values. In the average view, each of 12 equal tiles represents one monthly input, not an observed index magnitude. The reported 69.2 average is not recalculated.

Diffusion indexes describe reported breadth and direction, not inflation rates. The average milestone is highest since April 2023; the monthly index declined. No survey-driven Bitcoin move or policy decision is depicted.

ISM said tariffs and fuel costs were the most frequently cited supply-chain issues. Some respondents had not yet passed higher costs through to customers, leaving the eventual effect on consumer inflation uncertain.

The Federal Reserve held its target range at 3.75%-4% on Oct. 28, before the services release, and the central bank statement described inflation as elevated and said additional information would help determine the appropriate policy stance.

The possible benefit of softer activity runs through easier rate expectations and investors’ willingness to take risk. Persistent cost pressure complicates that route to relief, and establishing a change in financial conditions requires evidence of how markets price the outlook, beyond the survey itself.

An IMF working paper published in August 2023 found that US monetary tightening reduced a common crypto-price factor, meaning shared movements across crypto prices, through investors’ willingness to take risk, similar to the channel operating in equities.

That historical finding explains a plausible exposure, with any Bitcoin reaction to this particular survey requiring separate market evidence.

The next scheduled US checkpoint is the October employment report, due Nov. 6. As of Nov. 5, those results remain ahead and could change the balance between the growth and inflation concerns facing policymakers.

The test now is whether incoming data are followed by easier rate expectations and lower yields, which would strengthen the case for relief. Stronger Bitcoin buying without leverage could offer separate support through demand.

XIIlab Approves KRW 10B Convertible Bond to Expand AI Data Center Business

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

South Korean AI infrastructure company XIIlab disclosed in a November 6, 2026 regulatory filing that its board approved a KRW 10 billion privately placed convertible bond for its AI data-center business. Subscription and cash payment are scheduled for November 16, 2026, so the disclosure records an approved financing, not a completed raise.

XIIlab’s announcement connects the proceeds to working capital for GPU-cluster construction and a broader operating model. The company plans to apply its AstraGo software to infrastructure it builds, pursue operations and management contracts, and generate recurring revenue alongside construction work.

The first-series bonds are bearer, unsecured and privately placed, with the full amount classified as operating funds. Both the coupon and maturity interest rates are 0%, no separate interest is paid before maturity, and principal is repayable at 100% of the electronically registered amount on November 16, 2031. Bonds held to maturity therefore retain a full repayment obligation despite the absence of coupon and maturity interest.

Conversion is set at KRW 8,106 per share and would produce 1,233,654 registered common shares if exercised in full. Investors can request conversion from November 16, 2027 through October 16, 2031. The bonds exclude a share-price-decline refixing clause, although separate adjustments apply to specified corporate actions, including below-market share issuance, mergers, capital reductions, stock splits and consolidations.

XIIlab or its designee can call up to 50% of each investor’s original subscription, capped at KRW 5 billion in total, at quarterly dates from November 16, 2027 through November 16, 2028. The company presents that option as a way to reduce potential dilution if its share price rises. Investors hold a counterbalancing put: they can request early repayment at 100% from May 16, 2029 and at subsequent quarterly dates. Taken together, the provisions balance potential equity dilution against a company-side purchase option and preserve a creditor route to seek cash repayment before maturity.

The planned subscriptions comprise KRW 8 billion from National Growth Fund sub-funds managed by Korea Investment Value Asset Management, DB Asset Management, Susung Asset Management and Orion Asset Management, and KRW 2 billion from other Orion-managed funds. XIIlab characterizes the National Growth Fund as a policy vehicle created to foster advanced strategic industries including AI, semiconductors and biotechnology, and places its AI infrastructure business within the fund’s principal investment targets.

The spending schedule assigns KRW 5 billion to 2027 and KRW 5 billion to 2028 and later, all for AI data-center-related business. XIIlab said it is securing construction working capital ahead of expected demand for newer GPUs, including Vera Rubin, and intends to expand GPU-cluster construction in its role as an NVIDIA Elite partner.

XIIlab’s AstraGo product documentation describes a Kubernetes-based platform for allocating GPU resources, running training and inference workloads, and managing resources and workloads in one environment. It supports existing NVIDIA GPU servers and specified Furiosa and DeepX NPUs, multi-server clusters, and on-premises or closed-network deployments. Pricing combines an annual subscription license with a per-node server installation charge, giving the operations plan a subscription-based commercial structure.

XIIlab launched AstraGo 2.1 on April 1, 2026, saying the release partitions one physical GPU across isolated inference workloads, runs training and inference in the same cluster, prioritizes resources for real-time inference, and separates allocation and usage histories by organization or service in a multi-tenant structure. Those are the operational functions XIIlab plans to develop further as it adds data-center management work.

The financing connects XIIlab’s GPU-cluster construction pipeline with an AstraGo-based operations model built around annual subscriptions and continuing management work. The central question for its medium-term direction is whether that project activity becomes recurring operations revenue while the company manages the dilution and cash-repayment choices embedded in the bonds.

Palo Alto Networks Explores Clutch Security Acquisition

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TechCrunch reported on November 6, 2026 that Palo Alto Networks is in talks to acquire Clutch Security for $500 million in cash plus additional milestone-based payouts. The report describes discussions rather than a signed or completed acquisition, and neither company responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

The person familiar with the discussions said Clutch was approaching $10 million in annual recurring revenue and had most recently been valued at $85 million in January 2025. TechCrunch characterized the proposed cash price as roughly 50 times that recurring revenue.

Clutch’s own January 30, 2025 Series A announcement says SignalFire led the $20 million round, with participation from existing investors Lightspeed Venture Partners and Merlin Ventures, bringing total funding to $28.5 million. The proceeds were designated for go-to-market expansion, research and development including Identity Lineage and Zero Trust controls, and customer-success staffing.

Clutch defines non-human identities as API keys, secrets, service accounts, tokens and certificates used across cloud infrastructure, CI/CD pipelines and AI-driven systems. These credentials give software access to enterprise resources, making their permissions and lineage central to agent security.

Clutch says its discovery product inventories agents across endpoints, SaaS applications, self-hosted infrastructure and cloud environments. It then maps each agent to its deployer, tools, authentication identities and reachable resources, producing a single inventory spanning all four environment types.

Clutch says its agent guardrails extend policies across that chain, covering the person, agent, tools, identities and resources. Its examples include restricting access to production infrastructure, protected branches, MCP servers and stored credentials. Enforcement can allow a tool call in development while blocking it in production, and each policy trigger and enforcement decision is recorded with lineage context.

That control chain closely resembles capabilities described in Palo Alto Networks’ fiscal 2026 annual report. Its Idira platform secures human, agentic and machine identities, while its AI Agents Security offering discovers agents, assigns identity attributes, limits access to task-specific resources, records activity and can suspend or revoke access. Secure AI Agents also scans SaaS, cloud and developer environments, places agents in a central registry, applies gateway guardrails and issues short-lived permissions that are revoked when a task ends.

The annual report also connects that portfolio to a series of acquisitions. CyberArk formed the identity-security platform, Koi added agentic endpoint security and enhanced Prisma AIRS, Portkey enhanced its AI gateway capabilities, and Console is expected to deepen agentic capabilities in Cortex. Clutch’s focus on discovery, identity lineage and context-aware enforcement therefore intersects with an existing product buildout spanning identity, endpoints and AI gateways.

Cyera announced on September 3, 2026 that it had completed its $1 billion acquisition of Oasis Security, combining its data-security platform with Oasis’s non-human-identity access-management technology. Oasis, founded in 2022, developed continuous, policy-driven controls over which workloads and agents can access systems, in what context and for how long. Cyera said the platform would operate as Cyera Identity, its dedicated identity pillar.

The Oasis deal supplies a completed comparison point for consolidation in agent identity; it does not show that Clutch will be acquired. For now, the reported talks pair close technical overlap with no signed agreement. Whether Clutch’s focused controls become part of Palo Alto Networks’ expanding identity portfolio remains the central open question.

Local LLMs vs Cloud AI: Choosing the Right Solution for...

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at veduis.com on Nov 6

The explosion of large language model capabilities has created a fundamental choice for small businesses: rely on cloud AI services like ChatGPT and Claude, or run AI models locally on owned hardware. Each approach carries distinct implications for privacy, cost, performance, and operational complexity.

This comparison examines both options through the lens of small business requirements, providing the framework needed to make an informed decision based on specific circumstances rather than general hype.

Before diving into comparisons, clarity on what each approach actually means helps frame the discussion.

Cloud AI refers to large language models hosted by companies like OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Microsoft. Users send queries over the internet to remote servers where powerful hardware processes requests and returns responses. The model itself never touches the user's device.

Local LLMs are AI models that run entirely on hardware the user owns or controls. The model files download to local storage, and all processing happens on local CPUs, GPUs, or specialized AI accelerators. No data leaves the local environment during inference.

For many small businesses, privacy concerns drive the local LLM consideration more than any other factor.

When using cloud AI services, every prompt and response passes through third-party infrastructure:

OpenAI's business tier and API usage exclude data from training by default, but enterprise agreements provide stronger guarantees. Consumer ChatGPT usage may contribute to model training unless users opt out. Similar variations exist across providers.

For businesses handling client confidential information, healthcare data, legal documents, or proprietary processes, cloud AI usage requires careful evaluation of provider terms and potential liability.

Local deployment eliminates third-party data exposure entirely:

A law firm using AI to summarize case documents gains significant advantage from local deployment. Client privilege concerns that might prohibit cloud AI usage disappear when processing happens entirely on firm-controlled hardware.

Cost comparisons require honest accounting of all expenses, not just subscription fees.

Moderate usage with ChatGPT Team: $25 × 5 × 12 = $1,500/year

Heavy API usage (1M tokens/month): Approximately $300-500/month = $3,600-6,000/year

Local deployment requires upfront hardware investment with minimal ongoing costs.

A $2,000 hardware investment for medium-capability local LLM breaks even against $125/month cloud spending in 16 months. After break-even, local operation costs only electricity.

Raw capability differences between cloud and local options have narrowed significantly but remain relevant.

The largest, most capable models remain cloud-exclusive. GPT-4, Claude 3 Opus, and Gemini Ultra offer reasoning capabilities that exceed what runs efficiently on consumer hardware.

Cloud providers operate massive GPU clusters improved for inference. Response times typically range from 1-10 seconds for most queries, with streaming output beginning almost immediately.

Open-source models have improved dramatically. Meta's Llama 3 family, Mistral models, and community fine-tunes offer impressive capabilities:

Local performance depends entirely on hardware:

Larger models run proportionally slower. A 70B model might generate 10-20 tokens/second on high-end consumer hardware.

For businesses ready to examine local deployment, these tools provide the most accessible entry points.

Ollama provides the easiest path to running local LLMs. A single command downloads and runs models with sensible defaults.

Ollama exposes a local API compatible with many applications:

LM Studio offers a graphical interface for users preferring visual model management over command line.

Install Ollama or LM Studio on individual workstations. Each user maintains their own model installation. Simple but redundant.

Deploy models on a dedicated server accessible across the local network. All team members access the same instance:

Use local LLMs for sensitive processing while maintaining cloud access for tasks requiring peak capability. Route requests based on content sensitivity and complexity.

Different business scenarios favor different approaches.

Processing internal documents, contracts, meeting notes, and reports works excellently with local models. No external transmission, consistent performance, and 7B-13B models handle summarization well.

Developers working with proprietary codebases benefit from local code models like CodeLlama. IDE integrations like Continue work smoothly with local Ollama backends.

Parsing information from documents, emails, or databases involves potentially sensitive data. Local processing eliminates exposure concerns while capable models handle structured extraction tasks effectively.

Generating response drafts for customer inquiries allows local AI to help with routine communications while human review ensures quality before sending.

Tasks requiring deep reasoning, multi-step problem solving, or synthesis across broad knowledge domains benefit from the largest cloud models.

Marketing copy, blog posts, and creative writing often benefit from the nuanced language capabilities of frontier models.

Occasional research queries where peak capability matters more than privacy work well with cloud services.

Translation and multilingual content generation typically perform better on large cloud models trained on extensive multilingual data.

Use this framework to guide the cloud vs. local decision for specific business contexts.

The local LLM landscape evolves rapidly, with several trends worth monitoring.

New AI accelerators from Apple, AMD, and dedicated AI chip companies continue improving local inference performance. Hardware purchased today will run future improved models faster.

Research in model compression, quantization, and architecture improvements steadily closes the capability gap between what runs locally and cloud-exclusive models.

Commercial offerings for enterprise local AI deployment are emerging, providing managed solutions for businesses wanting local benefits with reduced operational overhead.

Increasing data privacy regulation may force certain industries toward local processing regardless of capability tradeoffs.

The choice between local and cloud AI is not permanent. Many businesses start with cloud services for immediate capability access, then migrate specific workloads to local deployment as use cases mature and privacy requirements clarify. The key is making an informed initial decision while remaining flexible as both technology and business needs evolve.

Related reading: NVIDIA Flare for local inference.

How we moved our agents from personal terminals into team channels

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at agentconnect.md

In six months, a team of under ten went from editor autocomplete to about thirty agents with standing jobs, from cluster checks and code review to releases, fork maintenance and customer support, plus the permissions and context sharing around them.

Over the past few months, AI has fundamentally changed how our team works. Much of our recurring work is now genuinely automated by agents: the daily cluster inspection and alert analysis, pull request review, maintenance of an upstream fork, release notes, and answering questions in customer groups. We are an engineering team of fewer than ten people.

A year ago, "using AI" meant an editor with AI in it: completions, rewriting a function, explaining unfamiliar code. Everyone used it, and nothing about how the team worked changed. The output was still a diff on someone's machine. Nobody else saw the conversation, and there was nothing in it worth seeing.

We did not build anything around it. The models were not yet at the point where you could hand them a task and walk away.

The turning point was Claude Opus 4.6. It was the first model we used that was worth building around: give it a task and it runs commands, reads the results and keeps going. Within weeks Claude Code and Codex were everyone's daily tools.

Then people started giving them work other than code. A production error: let an agent take the first look. An upstream release: let an agent upgrade our fork. Inspect the cluster every morning. Take customer questions. Within a month every engineer had accumulated a pile of their own glue: shell scripts, cron entries, a bot token in a dotfile, a prompt that stitched together the context the agent needed.

The first was duplication. When we compared what each of us had written, it was nearly the same: how messages get in and out, how to schedule, where credentials live, how to assemble context. Everyone had done it once, and each copy only ran on its owner's machine.

The second was lost context. The most useful part of a session is usually not the final diff but the process in the middle: what was tried, what failed, why this fix over that one, which dashboard had the answer. All of it sat in one person's terminal and was gone when the session ended. When someone else needed it, the only way to get it was a meeting.

In June we tried the most direct approach: a set of Slack bots built on the open-source OpenACP, changed so that each thread in an existing channel became one session.

Someone mentioned an agent in an incident thread, the rest of the team watched what it was checking, and asked follow-up questions in the same thread. Colleagues outside engineering started using the bots too; a mention in Slack is all it takes.

Then came two weeks of patches, all about threads: carrying the earlier thread into a mid-thread mention, backfilling messages the bot had missed, stopping bots from triggering each other in a loop. The software had no concept of "a conversation with several people and several bots in it", and we were bolting one on.

One platform app with several agents behind it, instead of one process, one Slack app and one set of tokens per bot

Separate identities for several users, instead of one shared login on one machine

A real control plane, instead of a bot that ran operational shell commands from the chat

Versioned daemons with controlled upgrades

Sessions that survive a restart, each with its own workspace, instead of one directory shared by every thread

Conversations and threads as core concepts, not adapter patches

We also tried replacing the whole thing with Hermes. Its demo covered most of what we wanted: loops, proactive messages, a kanban board and memory, all built in. We moved a rebase bot onto it and ran it for a day. The biggest problem was that it had no team layer: who may use which agent, who may see its sessions, which repositories and tools an agent may touch. There was no permission model; the whole thing assumed one user. Beyond that, its own harness and Claude Code ran interleaved inside one task, and without the coding agent's cache and resume, every turn re-sent the full context, so latency and cost were both high. When something failed, we could not tell which layer had failed. This describes the version we tried at the time; Hermes has changed a great deal since.

Two decisions from that time have not changed: permissions are part of the platform, not a feature to add later; and we keep the harness engineers already run locally, so that any behaviour seen in a channel can be reproduced in a terminal.

We built the connecting layer ourselves, on ACP, and open-sourced it a month later as AgentConnect.

The production organization was created on a Sunday in July. By Tuesday six agents were running, the same jobs people had been running from personal scripts, now each with a name, a channel and a machine. Today:

About thirty agents: one generalist, the rest specialists

Running on seven of our own machines plus a managed pool

Connected to Slack, Telegram, Discord and Linear

The generalist is connected to the development channel, direct messages and Linear. It can check out the team's repositories on demand, and anyone can mention it. Small changes are done right in the Slack thread: describe the problem, it edits the code, opens a pull request and runs the checks, and a person glances at it in the thread and merges, without opening a terminal.

Each specialist owns one kind of work, with its own repositories, tools, channels and schedules, and permissions scoped to that work.

Design documents and meetings. One agent owns documentation. Design discussions mention it in the development channel; it reads the current system and the existing design documents and proposes a path, colleagues add constraints in the thread, and it revises. Once the discussion settles, it opens a pull request that writes the conclusion into the matching design document under docs/, reviewed together with the implementation. Every meeting is transcribed. We do not use the transcription service's own summaries; the raw transcript goes into a separate repository, the agent writes the summary there, and then updates the related issues and design documents in the other repositories.

The documentation repository is organized by project, and each project has two parts, design documents and meetings:

Inspection and alerts. One agent walks the cluster every morning and posts a report to the alerts channel. A few others each watch one class of service in a business area for latency and versions. When an alert lands in the channel, the on-call agent picks it up in the same thread: it checks Grafana metrics, looks at pods and logs, and compares against recent changes in the repository. Kubernetes and Grafana are MCP services, registered once at the organization level and enabled per agent. How each class of event should be handled is written as a skill in the repository: which metrics to check first, the common causes, what it may handle on its own and when it must call a person. When the procedure changes, the skill changes, and every on-call agent uses the new one from the next run. Whether it is an incident is the engineer's call.

Cross-model code review. Every pull request gets a first pass from the review bot, which leaves inline comments and posts its own check. The same review bot can switch runtime and model per pull request, and different models notice different things. Running on Codex, it reproduced a bug in one pull request: editing a scheduled task's time before saving dropped the saved task's identity. It requested changes, and approved once the fix landed. Running on Claude, it approved another pull request and listed exactly what it had not verified and was leaving to CI. For a deeper look, someone mentions a reviewer on a stronger model.

Releases. Every merge to main produces a release candidate; a stable release points the release branch at a chosen commit. What happens after the release is the agent's job: confirm the release succeeded, read the pull requests between the two stable versions, group related pull requests into one feature entry, call out behaviour changes and upgrade notes separately, write that as the Summary at the top of the release page, and then comment on and close each issue the release fully fixed. The Summary on v2.8.0 came from this; the Features list below it is the generated changelog. The procedure is a skill, so the rules are the same whichever agent runs it.

The upstream fork. We maintain a fork of an upstream runtime adapter: the upstream tag plus a pile of our own patches. The patches carry logic, so when upstream refactors, the job is not resolving a few lines of conflict. It is understanding what upstream changed and what our patch was for, then re-implementing the intent on the new base. The whole thing goes to a coding agent: an upstream release or a schedule starts it, it reads both sides' changes, decides which patches are still needed, which upstream has absorbed and can be dropped, and which need rewriting, makes the changes in its own worktree, runs typecheck, tests and build, opens a pull request, and posts what needs a human judgement to the channel. That repository now has fourteen rebase branches, one per upstream version, with the check results beside each. Merging and publishing are done by a person.

Customer group support. A support agent answers questions in customers' Telegram groups and escalates what it cannot answer to an engineering channel in Slack. The groups get a lot of spam, so each message is scored first; only messages under the threshold reach the support agent, and the rest go to a moderator agent. Replies about a specific account are confirmed by a person before they go out.

The generalist can reach many repositories and sits in public channels, private channels and direct messages at once, so permissions cannot be skipped. We handle them in three layers.

The agent itself: who may change its configuration, which repositories and tools it may touch, who may use it in which channels, and which agents may call it or be called by it, each set separately.

Sessions: who may read a session follows the membership of the place the conversation happened. A session in a private channel is readable only by people currently in that channel; whoever leaves the channel can no longer read it. Direct messages stay private. There is no separate list to maintain.

Memory is layered by where the conversation happened: what was said somewhere is remembered there. What an agent learns in a public channel goes into the shared layer, a private or external group gets its own layer, and a direct message gets that person's layer. When it answers, the agent loads only the memory the current conversation is allowed to read.

Operations that need approval send an approval card by direct message to the agent's editors, rather than letting anyone in the channel approve.

What an agent produces has to be kept somewhere the team and the other agents can find later, with access still controlled by where it came from.

Design documents live in the same repository as the code, under docs/, with implementation status and links to issues and pull requests. The project instructions file says where the documents are and that a change to a design and its implementation go in the same pull request. The product repository has gone from 35 design documents at open-sourcing to 100 now. A reviewer reads the linked design document directly instead of asking the author to explain it in a meeting. Anything beyond a single repository, runbooks and architecture decisions, goes into the organization's knowledge library: owners publish it, it is versioned, and agents look it up on demand.

How to handle a class of alert, how to write a release description, how to rebase the fork: each is a skill file, imported once and enabled per agent. Once, after the agent that checks the price list every day posted its report, a colleague asked in the thread whether it had failed to find the matching skill. It confirmed it had not found it and had instead done a read-only comparison following the channel's earlier practice. The colleague pointed out the skill was in the repository. The agent found it, confirmed its earlier search had skipped hidden directories by default, and recorded the problem in its working conventions for later runs.

An agent's workspace is a Git repository, so what one agent writes, any agent's next session can read; the daemons on colleagues' own machines use the same repositories. Tickets are maintained by the agent doing the work: updated as it progresses, closed when done, linked to the documents and pull requests.

The local Claude Code and Codex memory mechanism is unchanged; we only point the memory directory at a Git repository, with hooks that pull at session start and push at session end, so it is the same memory across machines and harnesses. The platform agents' memory is a separate system, layered by conversation source as described above. Merging the two is something we may do later.

Today's agents are triggered by a mention, a schedule, a pull request or issue event, a webhook, or a judgement (is this spam, which agent should take it), and they do the one thing the trigger specifies.

Proactive agents. Make the agent more like a teammate: it checks the things it is responsible for and proposes before acting. For example, it notices that a just-merged pull request changed behaviour and the matching design document was not updated, and proposes a documentation pull request in the development channel; or the same question has come up three times this week in a customer group, and it proposes writing the answer into the docs. It acts only once a person approves. This is in preview.

Computer use. Some things have no API, only a desktop app or a browser. Register a machine as a Computer, grant it to specific agents, and they can take screenshots, click and type; a person watches the live screen in the console and can take over at any time. Also in preview.

Joining meetings. Today a meeting is recorded, transcribed and processed afterwards by the documentation agent. Further out, the agent joins the meeting as a participant: it can be asked questions during the meeting, and decisions get written back to design documents and issues on the spot rather than afterwards. This has not started.

AgentConnect is open source under Apache 2.0 and deploys with Docker Compose or Kubernetes. If you hit a problem, open an issue; if you find it useful, you are welcome to star the repository.

https://github.com/agentconnect-md/agentconnect

Two Paths to the Future: The Paradigm Shift Facing Every Developer

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The current conversation surrounding AI and software engineering is dominated by anxiety. We debate when AI will replace us, why our workloads are increasing despite automation, and how the junior developer funnel is changing. However, these discussions often assume one fundamental thing: that our ultimate goal as developers must always be to *remain employees of traditional corporations. * If AI systematically shifts the job market, the lesson we must learn isn't to panic. Instead, we must realize that a major crossroads has emerged. Every technologist must now consciously choose which path to follow and understand the tradeoffs involved.

*Path 1: The Corporate Specialist (Labor Leverage) * This is the traditional route. In this ecosystem, you adapt to becoming a system governor, architect, and debugger-of-last-resort. You focus on managing complex legacy codebases, integrating advanced architectures like WebMCP, and guiding armies of autonomous agents to deliver enterprise objectives.

The advantage of this path is structure, scale, and access to massive corporate resources. However, the macroeconomic reality is hard to ignore. If major enterprises use autonomous agents to massively downsize their engineering departments, systemic employment drops.

This creates a dangerous economic loop: if disposable income vanishes across the workforce, who will buy the products these automated companies create? Relying solely on corporate hiring pipelines means tying your career security to an increasingly volatile and unpredictable corporate lifecycle.

*Path 2: The Sovereign Developer (AI Leverage) * This path represents a shift toward true ownership and self-reliance. Instead of studying solely to fit into a company's pre-existing architecture, you study to build independent systems.

The very same AI capabilities that allow a corporation to run with fewer people also give a single individual the production power of an entire traditional IT department. Historically, solo developers were severely bottlenecked by time and specialization. You could write great backend logic, but you might struggle with UI design, marketing, or cloud infrastructure.

Today, autonomous agents act as the ultimate force multipliers, seamlessly filling those execution gaps. On this path, your goal isn't to look for a seat at the corporate table. Instead, you use AI to build what those companies do, but directly for yourself, your clients, or your local community. You shift your focus from selling your labor to owning your product.

*The Decision Before Us * The future of technology is no longer just about learning the next popular framework or programming language; it is about choosing your leverage model.

Do you want to optimize your skills to remain indispensable to a legacy corporation? Or do you want to use the democratization of AI tools to build your own independent ecosystem? Both paths require deep technical mastery, critical thinking, and intense dedication, but they lead to entirely different destinations.

We don't need to stop working or stop learning. We just need to decide who we are building for. The tools are entirely in your hands, and the choice is yours. Which path are you choosing?

Major tech stocks plunge as US midterms add to fears of AI slowdown

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of billions of dollars in market value has been wiped from major tech firms in a stock market rout after the US midterm elections sparked concerns of a slowdown in AI development.

Shares in firms across the tech and artificial intelligence sectors plummeted following Democratic wins after election campaigns run on slowing AI.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq composite stock index closed down by more than 5.5% to lose an estimated $2.5tn (Â£1.9tn) in market capitalisation, according to Dow Jones data, while other key US stock indexes also saw big losses in their worst days since 2025.

The sell-off comes on top of broader concerns about whether the huge amounts being spent on AI will pay off.

Democrats in races across the US campaigned to slow the onslaught of data centres and AI, and there were dozens of measures on ballots around the country aimed at restricting the development of the hubs crucial for powering the technology.

They won both the US Senate and House of Representatives in Tuesday's midterms, taking control of Congress for the rest of US President Donald Trump's presidency.

Texas' Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned heavily against data centres, pledging a moratorium on new construction in the sector.

In a social media post, Trump blamed the slide on his political opponents.

"The Stock Market suffered one of the Worst Days in History based on the results of the Election," he said.

"Remember, it wasn't Republicans, it was Dumocrats. Do not fear, however, we will soon, one way or the other, be BIGGER AND STRONGER THAN EVER BEFORE. MAKE AMERICA GREAT AGAIN!"

Some Democrats have argued that AI has grown too big without any safeguards or oversight and have promised, with their new power in Congress, to change that.

"Americans don't want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity's future. NATIONAL DATA CENTER MORATORIUM NOW," Democrat Senator Bernie Sanders said in a post on X on Thursday.

Shares in companies heavily exposed to AI data centres, including crucial chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers, fell sharply on Thursday, highlighting investor concerns that had already been growing.

Nvidia shares slid more than 9%, knocking more than $400bn off the firm's market value.

Other chipmakers also suffered, with AMD and Micron both dropping by more than 17%. Intel shares also dropped, down 10.3%, while memory card maker SanDisk plunged more than 22%.

The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks the performance of 30 of the largest companies involved in semiconductor production, fell by more than 12%.

Shares in Elon Musk's companies SpaceX and Tesla also plunged, falling 8% and nearly 11% respectively and shaving more than $300bn off the firms' market value on Thursday.

Musk had spent nearly $450m backing Republican campaigns, including at least $45m in support of Ken Paxton for the Texas senate seat, but Texas was turned blue in the Democratic tsunami on Tuesday night.

In total, the so-called magnificent seven tech companies, including Apple, Nvidia and Tesla, have shed a trillion dollars in value in a single trading day.

The stock falls also hit other major US stock indexes. The S&P 500 index fell by 4.4% by the end of trading, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased gains made on Wednesday to close down more than 3.4%.

The Nasdaq Composite fall was so large it is now in what market watchers call correction territory, which is where an index or company value falls at least 10% below its recent high.

Boebert, billionaires' tax and other midterms races still to watch

Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said the steep sell-off on the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 was sending shockwaves into other markets.

"Fresh panic over the AI trade has hit Wall Street hard," she said.

"Fears that the vast sums poured into AI infrastructure may not deliver the returns investors have been banking on are now colliding with the prospect of a sharp slowdown in data centre construction."

Some tech leaders have said they were caught off guard by the backlash against data centres in the midterms.

OpenAI president and co-founder Greg Brockman said he believed everyone had been surprised by the reaction to data centres, and more work needed to be done to ensure communities were on board.

"A lot of our shift over the course of this year has really been to realise that from San Francisco trying to build infrastructure in Texas, Georgia, and other places, you can't just tweet and say: 'This is going to be good.' You need to be in those communities," Brockman said at the WSJ Tech conference in Napa, California on Wednesday evening.

Meta's president Dina Powell McCormick said that the industry needed to put more effort into selling AI as a good idea.

"I think we need to double and triple down on telling the story," she told the WSJ conference.

Alongside the immediate impacts of the US mid-term elections, some investors have become concerned that AI shares could be in a bubble that is due a correction.
